\{x_{1}^{\prime\prime},\dots x_{l}^{\prime\prime}\}=S^{\prime}
.
Obviously we have
x^{C}=x^{\prime C}
.
By the induction hypothesis, we have
ADSub(x_{i})=ADSub(x^{\prime}_{i})
for each
x_{i}
.
From this, we can infer that
ADSub(x)=ADSub(x^{\prime})
also holds.
By the induction hypothesis we also have
x^{\prime}_{i}\in I_{1}\setminus I_{0}
for each
x^{\prime}_{i}
.
From this we can infer that
S^{\prime}\subseteq in(L)\cup\{a\}
must hold.
By construction of
L_{a}
, we now have
x^{\prime}\in I_{1}\setminus I_{0}
as required.
∎

Next, we show that propagated labelings will be admissible if three conditions are met:
First, we need to start with an already admissible labeling
L
.
Secondly, for the argument
a
that we choose as the
“starting point”
for our propagation, we need
L(a)\neq\textit{OUT}
and for all attackers
b
of
a
we need to have
L(b)=\textit{OUT}
.
Lastly, we need to ensure that there does not exist a support chain
\mathcal{C}=\big\{(S_{0},b_{0}),\dots,(S_{n},b_{n})\big\}
starting at
a\in S_{0}
s.t. we have
S_{i}\setminus\{b_{i-1}\}\subseteq in(L)
, while there exists
(c,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow}
with
L(c)\neq\textit{OUT}
.
We will later ensure that these conditions are always satisfied when creating a propagated labeling
L_{a}
.

To make the actual proof more accessible, we have split it into several parts.
We begin by showing that
L_{a}
is a labeling:

Proposition 13
.

Let
\mathcal{J}=(\mathcal{A},{\rightarrow},{\Rightarrow},{\preceq})
be a JSBAF, let
L\in adm(\mathcal{J})
be an admissible labeling of
\mathcal{J}
and let
a\in\mathcal{A}
be an argument.
Furthermore, assume that
L(a)\neq\textit{OUT}
and that for all attackers
b
of
a
, we have
L(b)=\textit{OUT}
.
Lastly, assume that there does not exist a support chain
\big\{(S_{0},b_{0}),...,(S_{n},b_{n})\big\}\subseteq{\Rightarrow}
and an attack
(c,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow}
with
a\in S_{0}
,
L(c)\neq\textit{OUT}
,
S_{0}\setminus\{a\}\subseteq in(L)
and
S_{i}\setminus\{b_{i-1}\}\subseteq in(L)
for
0<i\leq n
.
Then
L_{a}
is a labeling.

Proof.

It is easy to see that each argument gets
some
label and that
undec(L_{a})\cap in(L_{a})=undec(L_{a})\cap out(L_{a})=\emptyset
.
Therefore, we have left to show that
in(L_{a})\cap out(L_{a})=\emptyset
also holds.
Towards a contradiction, assume that there is an argument
x\in out(L_{a})\cap in(L_{a})
.
We show the contradiction by induction over
n\in\mathbb{N}
for
x\in O_{n}
.

Induction start,
n=0
:
This means there exists some
(y,x)\in{\rightarrow}
with
y\in in(L_{a})
.
We make two case distinctions, based on the origins of
x\in in(L_{a})
and
y\in in(L_{y})
:
We have
x\in in(L_{a})
either because
x\in I_{0}
or because
x\in I_{k}
for some
k\geq 1
.
Similarly, we have
y\in in(L_{a})
either because
y\in I_{0}
or because
y\in in(I_{k})
for some
k\geq 1
.

Assume first that
x\in I_{0}
and
y\in I_{0}
holds:
If we have
x\in I_{0}
because
x\in in(L)
, then we can use admissibility of
L
to infer that
L(y)=\textit{OUT}
.
On the other hand, if we have
x\in I_{0}
because
x=a
, then we can use our assumption that all attackers of
a
are labeled
OUT
in
L
to infer
L(y)=\textit{OUT}
.
Either way, we have
y\in out(L)\cap I_{0}
, a contradiction.

Now assume that
x\in I_{0}
and
y\in I_{k}
for some
k\geq 1
:
As we have dealt with the case
y\in I_{0}
above, we assume
y\in I_{k}\setminus I_{0}
.
By Proposition
12

there exists an argument
y^{\prime}\in I_{1}\setminus I_{0}
s.t.
y^{\prime C}=y^{C}
and
ADSub(y^{\prime})=ADSub(y)
.
Because
y^{\prime}\in I_{1}\setminus I_{0}
, we can infer from the construction of
I_{1}
that there exists a support
(S,y^{\prime})\in{\Rightarrow}
with
a\in S
and
S\setminus\{a\}\subseteq in(L)
.
Similar to the case of
y
above, we have
L(y^{\prime})=\textit{OUT}
, because all attackers of
x
are labeled
OUT
in
L
and
y^{\prime}
attacks
x
.
Now we have the support
(S,y^{\prime})
with
L(y^{\prime})=\textit{OUT}
and
S\setminus\{a\}\subseteq in(L)
.
Because
L
was an admissible labeling, we can now infer that
L(a)=\textit{OUT}
must hold, contradicting our assumption
L(a)\neq\textit{OUT}
.

Next, let us consider the cases where
x\in I_{k}
for some
k\geq 1
.
We assume
x\not\in I_{0}
as we have dealt with this case before.
We again use Proposition
12
to infer that there exists an argument
x^{\prime}\in I_{1}\setminus I_{0}
s.t.
ADSub(x)=ADSub(x^{\prime})
and
x^{C}=x^{\prime C}
.
Next, consider the attack
(y,x)
:
If
(y,x)\in{\rightarrow}
because
y
undercuts
x
, then
y
also undercuts
x^{\prime}
.
On the other hand, if
y
gen-rebuts
x
, then we can use Proposition
11
to infer the existence of an argument
\widetilde{y}:y\rightarrow\lnot\bigwedge ADSub(x^{C})
.
Because
ADSub(x)=ADSub(x^{\prime})
, we now also infer
(\widetilde{y},x^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow}
.

Now, let us consider the origin of
y\in in(L_{a})
again:
We either have
y\in I_{0}
or
y\in I_{k}
for some
k\geq 1
.
Assume first that
y\in I_{0}
, i.e.
y\in in(L)
or
y=a
.
Note that, because
x^{\prime}\in I_{1}\setminus I_{0}
, there must exist a support
(S_{x},x^{\prime})\in{\Rightarrow}
s.t.
a\in S_{x}
and
S_{x}\setminus\{a\}\subseteq in(L)
.
By our assumption on the support chains starting at
a
, for this support chain
\big\{(S_{x},x^{\prime})\big\}\subseteq{\Rightarrow}
, we must have
L(y)=\textit{OUT}
(if
y
undercuts
x
) or
L(\widetilde{y})=\textit{OUT}
(if
y
gen-rebuts
x
), which in turn implies
L(y)=\textit{OUT}
by admissibility of
L
and construction of
\widetilde{y}
.
Either way, we can infer
L(y)=\textit{OUT}
, which contradicts
y\in in(L)\cup\{a\}
.

Now, assume that
y\in I_{k}\setminus I_{0}
for some
k\geq 1
.
We use Proposition
12
again to infer:
If
y
undercuts
x
, then there exists an argument
y^{\prime}\in I_{1}\setminus I_{0}
s.t.
y^{\prime C}=y^{C}
and
ADSub(y^{\prime})=ADSub(y)
and if
y
gen-rebuts
x
, then there exists an argument
\widetilde{y}^{\prime}\in I_{1}\setminus I_{0}
s.t.
\widetilde{y}^{\prime C}=\widetilde{y}^{C}
and
ADSub(\widetilde{y}^{\prime})=ADSub(\widetilde{y})
.
Note that, because
y^{\prime}\in I_{1}\setminus I_{0}
(respectively
\widetilde{y}^{\prime}\in I_{1}\setminus I_{0}
), we can infer that there exists a support
(S_{y},y^{\prime})\in{\Rightarrow}
(respectively
(S_{y},\widetilde{y}^{\prime})\in{\Rightarrow}
) with
a\in S_{y}
and
S_{y}\setminus\{a\}\subseteq in(L)
.
Because
ADSub(y^{\prime})=ADsub(y)
(respectively
ADSub(\widetilde{y}^{\prime})=ADSub(\widetilde{y})
), and
y^{\prime C}=y^{C}
(respectively
\widetilde{y}^{\prime C}=\widetilde{y}^{C}
), we can again infer that
(y^{\prime},x^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow}
(respectively
(\widetilde{y}^{\prime},x^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow}
) holds.
By the assumptions made on the support chains starting at
a
, we can now again infer that
L(y^{\prime})=\textit{OUT}
(respectively
L(\widetilde{y}^{\prime})=\textit{OUT}
).
However, for the supporting set
S_{y}
we can now use the admissibility of
L
and the fact that
S_{y}\setminus\{a\}\subseteq in(L)
to infer
L(a)=\textit{OUT}
, which contradicts our original assumption
L(a)\neq\textit{OUT}
.
This concludes the induction start.

Induction step,
n\rightsquigarrow n+1
:
By the induction hypothesis we have
O_{n}\cap in(L_{a})=\emptyset
, therefore we restrict ourselves to
O_{n+1}\setminus O_{n}
.
Assume that
x\in O_{n+1}\setminus O_{n}
.
This means there exists some support
(S,c)\in{\Rightarrow}
, with
x\in S
,
S\setminus\{x\}\subseteq in(L_{a})
and
c\in O_{n}
.
By assumption,
x\in in(L_{a})
also holds.
Now we can infer that there must be some
k\in\mathbb{N}
for which we have
S\subseteq I_{k}
.
This implies
c\in I_{k+1}\subseteq in(L_{a})
by construction of
in(L_{a})
.
Now
c\in O_{n}\cap in(L_{a})
, which contradicts the induction hypothesis.
∎

Next, we show that arguments accepted in
L_{a}
are also legally accepted:

Proposition 14
.

Let
\mathcal{J}=(\mathcal{A},{\rightarrow},{\Rightarrow},{\preceq})
be a JSBAF, let
L\in adm(\mathcal{J})
be an admissible labeling of
\mathcal{J}
and let
a\in\mathcal{A}
be an argument.
Furthermore, assume that
L(a)\neq\textit{OUT}
and that for all attackers
b
of
a
, we have
L(b)=\textit{OUT}
.
Lastly, assume that there does not exist a support chain
\big\{(S_{0},b_{0}),...,(S_{n},b_{n})\big\}\subseteq{\Rightarrow}
and an attack
(c,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow}
with
a\in S_{0}
,
L(c)\neq\textit{OUT}
,
S_{0}\setminus\{a\}\subseteq in(L)
and
S_{i}\setminus\{b_{i-1}\}\subseteq in(L)
for
0<i\leq n
.
If
x\in in(L_{a})
, then
x
is legally IN w.r.t.
L_{a}
.

Proof.

First, let us consider the attackers of
x
:
Assume that
x\in in(L_{a})
and
(y,x)\in{\rightarrow}
.
If
x\in I_{0}
, then we either have
x\in in(L)
or
x=a
.
In both cases, we know
L(y)=\textit{OUT}
.
Since
in(L)\subseteq in(L_{a})
, we can infer that
L_{a}(y)=\textit{OUT}
holds due to the same attack or support chain that lead to
L(y)=\textit{OUT}
.
Now assume
x\in I_{k}\setminus I_{0}
for some
k\geq 1
.
By Proposition
12
, there exists
x^{\prime}\in I_{1}\setminus I_{0}
s.t.
x^{\prime C}=x^{C}
and
ADSub(x^{\prime})=ADSub(x)
.
As for the proof that showed
L_{a}
is a labeling, we again want to point out that
x^{\prime}\in I_{1}\setminus I_{0}
implies the existence of a support
(S,x^{\prime})\in{\Rightarrow}
with
a\in S
and
S\setminus\{a\}\subseteq in(L)
.
Now for the attack
(y,x)\in{\rightarrow}
:
If
y
undercuts
x
, then
(y,x^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow}
also holds.
On the other hand, if
y
gen-rebuts
x
, then we can again use Proposition
11
to infer the existence of an argument
\widetilde{y}:y\rightarrow\lnot\bigwedge ADSub(x)^{C}
and because
ADSub(x)=ADSub(x^{\prime})
and
DR(x)=DR(x^{\prime})
, we have
(\widetilde{y},x^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow}
.
Since
x\in I_{1}
and by the assumptions made on support-chains starting at
a
, we can infer:
If
y
undercuts
x
, then
L(y)=\textit{OUT}
must thold.
On the other hand, if
y
gen-rebuts
x
, then
L(\widetilde{y})=\textit{OUT}
must hold and by admissibility of
L
this implies
L(y)=\textit{OUT}
.
In both cases, we have
L(y)=\textit{OUT}
and can again infer
L_{a}(y)=\textit{OUT}
due to the same attack or support chain that lead to
L(y)=\textit{OUT}
.

Lastly, for the supports
(S,b)\in{\Rightarrow}
with
x\in S
:
By construction of
in(L_{a})
, we cannot have
S\subseteq in(L_{a})
while
b\not\in in(L_{a})
.
Furthermore, if
|S\setminus in(L_{a})|=1
and
L_{a}(b)=\textit{OUT}
, then
S\setminus in(L_{a})\subseteq out(L_{a})
by construction of
out(L_{a})
.
From this we can infer that, if
x\preceq c
for all
c\in S\setminus\{x\}
, then one of the items
1.a
to
1.c
of Definition
2
holds, as required.
∎

Before we continue with the actual proof, we show a small auxiliary statement which tells us that, if we have an admissible labeling
L
and two arguments
a
and
a^{\prime}
, where the defeasible rules and the conclusions of sub-arguments of
a^{\prime}
are also defeasible rules and conclusions of sub-arguments of
a
, then
L(a^{\prime})=\textit{OUT}
implies
L(a)=\textit{OUT}
:

Proposition 15
.

Let
\mathcal{J}=(\mathcal{A},{\rightarrow},{\Rightarrow},{\preceq})
be a JSBAF and let
L\in adm(\mathcal{J})
be an admissible labeling of
\mathcal{J}
.
Furthermore, let
a,a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
be two arguments s.t.
DR(a^{\prime})\subseteq DR(a)
and
ADSub(a^{\prime})^{C}\subseteq ADSub(a)^{C}
.
Then
L(a^{\prime})=\textit{OUT}
implies
L(a)=\textit{OUT}
.

Proof.

We go through the possible cases for why
a^{\prime}
is rejected in
L
:
Suppose first that we have
L(a^{\prime})=\textit{OUT}
due to an attack
(b,a^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow}
with
L(b)=\textit{IN}
.
If this attack is the result of an undercut, then we can use
DR(a^{\prime})\subseteq DR(a)
to infer that
b
also undercuts
a
, meaning
(b,a^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow}
also holds.
By admissiblity of
L
, this implies
L(a)=\textit{OUT}
.

Next, suppose this attack is the result of a gen-rebut.
By Proposition
11
, there exists an argument
b^{\prime}
which is of the form
b^{\prime}:b\rightarrow\lnot\bigwedge ADSub(a^{\prime})^{C}
.
By admissibility of
L
, we have
L(b^{\prime})=\textit{IN}
.
Since
ADSub(a^{\prime})^{C}\subseteq ADSub(a)^{C}
, we can infer that
b^{\prime}
gen-rebuts
a
.
Because
(b,a^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow}
by assumption, we have
b\not\prec a^{\prime}
.
Since
DR(b)=DR(b^{\prime})
, while
DR(a^{\prime})\subseteq DR(a)
, we can infer that
b^{\prime}\not\prec a
holds.
Now
(b^{\prime},a)\in{\rightarrow}
and by admissibility of
L
, we have
L(a)=\textit{OUT}
.

Lastly, assume that
L(a^{\prime})=\textit{OUT}
due to a support chain
\mathcal{C}=\big\{(S_{0},b_{0}),\dots,(S_{n},b_{n})\big\}
and an attack
(c,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow}
with
a^{\prime}\in S_{0}
,
L(c)=\textit{IN}
,
b_{i}\in out(L)
for
0\leq i\leq n
,
S_{0}\setminus\{a^{\prime}\}\subseteq in(L)
and
S_{i}\setminus\{b_{i-1}\}\subseteq in(L)
for
0<i\leq n
.
Let
S=\big(S_{0}\setminus\{a^{\prime}\}\big)\cup\bigcup\limits_{0<i\leq n}\big(S_{i}\setminus\{b_{i-1}\}\big)
, i.e.
S
contains all the
IN
-labeled arguments of the support-chain
\mathcal{C}
.
First, assume that the attack
(c,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow}
is the result of an undercut.
Then this attack must also be directed towards one of the arguments in
S\cup\{a\}
.
By assumption,
L(c)=\textit{IN}
,
L
is an admissible lableing and
S\subseteq in(L)
.
From this we can infer that
(c,a)\in{\rightarrow}
must hold.
By admissibility of
L
this implies
L(a)=\textit{OUT}
.
Next, assume that
(c,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow}
is the result of a gen-rebut.
We use Proposition
11
to infer that there exists an attack
(c^{\prime},b_{n})\in{\rightarrow}
, where
c^{\prime}
is of the form
c^{\prime}:c\rightarrow\lnot\bigwedge ADSub(b_{n})^{C}
.
Now we construct the argument
b^{\prime}
as follows:
b^{\prime}:a,b_{0}^{\prime},\dots,b_{m}^{\prime}\rightarrow\bigwedge\{a,b_{0}^{\prime},\dots,b_{m}^{\prime}\}^{C}
, where
\{b_{0}^{\prime},\dots,b_{m}^{\prime}\}=S
.
We want to point out three facts for the argument
b^{\prime}
:
Firstly, we know that
\{b_{0}^{\prime},\dots,b_{m}^{\prime}\}=S\subseteq in(L)
holds.
Secondly, from
ADSub(a^{\prime})^{C}\subseteq ADSub(a)^{C}
, we can infer
ADSub(b_{n})^{C}\subseteq ADSub(b^{\prime})^{C}
.
This means
c^{\prime}
gen-rebuts
b^{\prime}
.
Thirdly, we have
DR(b_{n})\subseteq DR(b^{\prime})
.
By assumption,
(c,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow}
is the result of a gen-rebut.
This means we have
c^{\prime}\not\prec b_{n}
(since
DR(c)=DR(c^{\prime})
), which implies
c^{\prime}\not\prec b^{\prime}
.
Now we have
(c^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow}
.
By admissibility of
L
and by construction of
c^{\prime}
, we have
L(c^{\prime})=\textit{IN}
.
This means
b^{\prime}
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L
, and by admissibility of
L
we can infer
L(b^{\prime})=\textit{OUT}
.
Since
\{b_{0}^{\prime},\dots,b_{m}^{\prime}\}=S\subseteq in(L)
, this implies
a\in out(L)
, otherwise none of the arguments in
S
would be legally
IN
w.r.t.
L
.
This proves the claim.
∎

Now we can continue with our actual proof of the admissibility of
L_{a}
.
In the next step, we show that an argument is rejected in
L_{a}
iff it is legally rejected:

Proposition 16
.

Let
\mathcal{J}=(\mathcal{A},{\rightarrow},{\Rightarrow},{\preceq})
be a JSBAF, let
L\in adm(\mathcal{J})
be an admissible labeling of
\mathcal{J}
and let
a\in\mathcal{A}
be an argument.
Furthermore, assume that
L(a)\neq\textit{OUT}
and that for all attackers
b
of
a
, we have
L(b)=\textit{OUT}
.
Lastly, assume that there does not exist a support chain
\big\{(S_{0},b_{0}),...,(S_{n},b_{n})\big\}\subseteq{\Rightarrow}
and an attack
(c,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow}
with
a\in S_{0}
,
L(c)\neq\textit{OUT}
,
S_{0}\setminus\{a\}\subseteq in(L)
and
S_{i}\setminus\{b_{i-1}\}\subseteq in(L)
for
0<i\leq n
.
We have
x\in out(L_{a})
iff
x
is legally OUT w.r.t.
L_{a}
.

Proof.

\leftarrow
:
First, assume that
x
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{a}
due to an attack.
Then
x\in O_{0}\subseteq out(L_{a})
.
Next, assume
x
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{a}
due to a support
(S,b)
with
x\in S
and
b\in out(L_{a})
.
By construction of
L_{a}
, there needs to exist some
n\in\mathbb{N}
s.t.
b\in O_{n}
.
Now
x\in O_{n+1}\subseteq out(L_{a})
as required.

\rightarrow
:
We need to show that, if
x\in out(L_{a})
, then
x
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{a}
.
Note that, if
x\in O_{0}
, then this is trivial.
We therefore assume
x\not\in O_{0}
.
By the construction of
O_{k+1}
from
O_{k}
, it is clear that, if
x\in O_{k+1}\setminus O_{k}
, then there exists a support chain
\mathcal{C}=\big\{(S_{0},b_{0}),\dots,(S_{n},b_{n})\big\}\subseteq{\Rightarrow}
and an attack
(y,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow}
, s.t.
x\in S_{0}
,
L_{a}(y)=\textit{IN}
, for
0\leq i\leq n
we have
b_{i}\in out(L_{a})
, for
0<i\leq n
we have
S_{i}\setminus\{b_{i}-1\}\subseteq in(L_{a})
and for
S_{0}
we have
S_{0}\setminus\{x\}\subseteq in(L_{a})
(i.e. items
2.a
to
2.d
of Definition
2
all hold).
However, from the construction of
O_{k+1}
it is not clear that
x\preceq d
for all
d\in S\setminus\{x\}
holds (i.e. item
2.e
of Definition
2
) and in fact this is not necessarily the case.
Thus, for the cases where item
2.e
of Definition
2
does not hold, we will show that there is an
“alternative reason”
for
x
to be legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{a}
.

Suppose that
x\in O_{k+1}\setminus O_{k}
due to the support chain
\mathcal{C}=\big\{(S_{0},b_{0}),\dots,(S_{n},b_{n})\big\}
and an attack
(y,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow}
as described above.
Furthermore, assume that there is some
d\in S_{0}\setminus\{x\}
s.t.
d\prec x
(i.e. because of
d
, the support chain
\mathcal{C}
is no reason for
x
to be considered legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{a}
).
Let
S=\big(S_{0}\setminus\{x\}\big)\cup\bigcup\limits_{0<i\leq n}\big(S_{i}\setminus\{b_{i-1}\}\big)
, i.e.
S
contains all arguments labeled
IN
in the support chain
\mathcal{C}
.
We now claim that, for
S=\{z_{0},\dots,z_{m}\}
, we have
ADSub(x)^{C}\vDash_{C}\lnot\bigwedge\big(ADSub(y)\cup ADSub(z_{0})\cup\dots\cup ADSub(z_{m})\big)^{C}
.
To see that this claim holds, assume towards a contradiction that it does not.
Then there exists an interpretation
I
s.t.
I\vDash_{M}ADSub(x)^{C}
,
I\vDash_{M}ADSub(z_{i})^{C}
for each
z_{i}\in S
and
I\vDash_{M}ADSub(y)^{C}
.
Let
y^{C}=\lnot\bigwedge\Gamma
for some
\Gamma\subseteq Sub(b_{n})^{C}
.
From
I\vDash_{M}ADSub(x)^{C}
and
I\vDash_{M}ADSub(z_{i})^{C}
for each
z_{i}\in S
, we can infer that
I\vDash_{M}\bigwedge\Gamma
holds.
However, from
I\vDash_{M}ADSub(y)^{C}
, we can infer
I\vDash_{M}\lnot\bigwedge\Gamma
, a contradiction.

Next, we construct the arguments
y^{\prime}:y,z_{0},\dots,z_{m}\rightarrow\bigwedge\{y,z_{0},\dots,z_{m}\}^{C}
and
x^{\prime}:x_{0},\dots,x_{k}\rightarrow\lnot\bigwedge\Delta
, where
\{x_{0},\dots x_{k}\}=ADSub(x)
and
\Delta=\big(ADSub(y)\cup ADSub(z_{0})\cup\dots\cup ADSub(z_{m})\big)^{C}
.
Now
x^{\prime}
gen-rebuts
y^{\prime}
.
Note that, because
d\prec x
by assumption, there exists
r_{d}\in DR(d)\subseteq DR(y^{\prime})
s.t. for all
r_{x}\in DR(x)=DR(x^{\prime})
,
r_{d}\leq_{r}r_{x}
.
This implies
y^{\prime}\preceq_{ewl}x^{\prime}
, therefore
x^{\prime}\not\prec_{ewl}y^{\prime}
and thus
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow}
.
Because
y\in in(L_{a})
and
z_{0},\dots,z_{m}\subseteq in(L_{a})
, we can infer that
y^{\prime}\in in(L_{a})
holds by construction of
L_{a}
.
This implies that either
y^{\prime}\in I_{0}
or, by Proposition
12
, there exists
y^{\prime\prime}\in I_{1}\setminus I_{0}
s.t.
ADSub(y^{\prime})=ADSub(y^{\prime\prime})
and
y^{\prime C}=y^{\prime\prime C}
.
In the first case we have
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow}
and can infer
L(x^{\prime})=\textit{OUT}
from
y^{\prime}\in I_{0}
.
In the second case we have
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime})\in{\rightarrow}
because
ADSub(y^{\prime})=ADSub(y^{\prime\prime})
.
Furthermore, we can use
y^{\prime\prime}\in I_{1}\setminus I_{0}
to infer that there exists a support
(S^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime})\in{\Rightarrow}
with
a\in S^{\prime\prime}
and
S^{\prime\prime}\setminus\{a\}\subseteq in(L)
.
Now the support chain
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}=\big\{(S^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime})\big\}\subseteq{\Rightarrow}
is a support chain of which we know by assumption that all attackers of
y^{\prime\prime}
are labeled
OUT
in
L
.
With this we can again infer
L(x^{\prime})=\textit{OUT}
.

By construction of
x^{\prime}
, we obviously have
DR(x^{\prime})\subseteq DR(x)
and
ADSub(x^{\prime})^{C}\subseteq ADSub(x)^{C}
.
This means we can apply Proposition
15
to infer that
L(x)=\textit{OUT}
must thold.
By assumption,
L
is an admissible labeling.
This means
x
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L
, either due to an attack or due to a support chain.
Since
in(L)\subseteq in(L_{a})
, we can now infer that
x
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{a}
due to the same attack or support chain.
This finishes the proof.
∎

Finally, we are ready to combine the previous propositions to show that
L_{a}
is an admissible labeling:

Proposition 17
.

Let
\mathcal{J}=(\mathcal{A},{\rightarrow},{\Rightarrow},{\preceq})
be a JSBAF, let
L\in adm(\mathcal{J})
be an admissible labeling of
\mathcal{J}
and let
a\in\mathcal{A}
be an argument.
Furthermore, assume that
L(a)\neq\textit{OUT}
and that for all attackers
b
of
a
, we have
L(b)=\textit{OUT}
.
Lastly, assume that there does not exist a support chain
\big\{(S_{0},b_{0}),...,(S_{n},b_{n})\big\}\subseteq{\Rightarrow}
and an attack
(c,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow}
with
a\in S_{0}
,
L(c)\neq\textit{OUT}
,
S_{0}\setminus\{a\}\subseteq in(L)
and
S_{i}\setminus\{b_{i-1}\}\subseteq in(L)
for
0<i\leq n
.
Then the propagated labeling
L_{a}
is an admissible labeling.

Proof.

By Proposition
13
we know that
L_{a}
is a labeling.
By Proposition
14
we know that
x\in in(L_{a})
implies
x
is legally
IN
w.r.t.
L_{a}
.
By Proposition
16
we know that
x\in out(L_{a})
iff
x
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{a}
.
Lastly, because
L\in adm(\mathcal{J})
, it is clear from the construction of
L_{a}
that
STR_{\mathcal{J}}\subseteq in(L_{a})
holds.
We conclude that
L_{a}
is an admissible labeling.
∎

Next, we use propagated labelings to show that preferred labelings are closed under sub-arguments.

Lemma 2
.

Let
\mathcal{J}=(\mathcal{A},{\rightarrow},{\Rightarrow},{\preceq})
be a JSBAF and let
L\in pr(\mathcal{J})
be a preferred labeling of
\mathcal{J}
.
Then for all
a\in in(L)
, we have
Sub(a)\subseteq in(L)
.

Proof.

Towards a contradiction, suppose that the claim does not hold.
Then there is
a^{\prime}\in Sub(a)\setminus\{a\}
s.t.
a^{\prime}\not\in in(L)
.
We claim that all attackers of
a^{\prime}
are labeled
OUT
by
L
and that there does not exist a support chain
\mathcal{C}=\big\{(S_{0},b_{0}),...,(S_{n},b_{n})\big\}
and an attack
(c,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow}
s.t.
a^{\prime}\in S_{0}
,
L(c)\neq\textit{OUT}
,
S_{0}\setminus\{a^{\prime}\}\subseteq in(L)
and
S_{i}\setminus\{b_{i-1}\}\subseteq in(L)
for
0<i\leq n
.
If this holds, then by Proposition
17
the propagated labeling
L_{a^{\prime}}
is an admissible labeling.
Since
a^{\prime}\not\in in(L)
by assumption, this implies
in(L)\subset in(L_{a^{\prime}})
, contradicting the assumption that
L
is a preferred labeling.

Now for the attackers of
a^{\prime}
and the support chains
\mathcal{C}=\big\{(S_{0},b_{0}),...,(S_{n},b_{n})\big\}
with
a^{\prime}\in S_{0}
:
From the way arguments and attacks are constructed in an AS and from the translation of an AS into a JSBAF, it is clear that any attacker of
a^{\prime}
is also an attacker of
a
.
Since
a\in in(L)
by assumption, we can infer that
a
is legally
IN
, meaning all attackers of
a
(and therefore all attackers of
a^{\prime}
) are labeled
OUT
.

Next, for the support chains starting at
a^{\prime}
:
Towards a contradiction, assume that there is a support chain
\mathcal{C}=\big\{(S_{0},b_{0}),...,(S_{n},b_{n})\big\}
and an attack
(c,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow}
s.t.
a^{\prime}\in S_{0}
,
L(c)\neq\textit{OUT}
,
S_{0}\setminus\{a^{\prime}\}\subseteq in(L)
and
S_{i}\setminus\{b_{i-1}\}\subseteq in(L)
for
0<i\leq n
.
Note that, if
c
is undercutting
b_{n}
, then it is also undercutting
a^{\prime}
or some argument
x\in\bigcup\limits_{0\leq i\leq n}\big(S_{i}\cap in(L)\big)
.
As we have argued above, if the undercut is directed towards
a^{\prime}
, then it is also directed towards
a
.
Since
a
is legally
IN
but
L(c)\neq\textit{OUT}
, this assumption leads to a contradiction.
We therefore assume that
c
is not undercutting
a^{\prime}
but some other argument
x\in\bigcup\limits_{0\leq i\leq n}\big(S_{i}\cap in(L)\big)
.
However, if
c
is undercutting such an argument
x
, then
L(c)\neq\textit{OUT}
implies
x\not\in in(L)
by admissibility of
L
.
This is also a contradiction, therefore we assume that
c
is not undercutting
b_{n}
, which means
c
must be gen-rebutting
b_{n}
.
By Proposition
11
, there now exists
c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}
which is of the form
c^{\prime}:c\rightarrow\lnot\bigwedge ADSub(b_{n})^{C}
.
This means
c^{\prime}
also gen-rebuts
b_{n}
.
For the argument
c
, we have
c\not\prec b_{n}
because
(c,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow}
by assumption and because this attack results from a gen-rebut.
Since
DR(c)=DR(c^{\prime})
, we can infer that
c^{\prime}\not\prec b_{n}
also holds, meaning
(c^{\prime},b_{n})\in{\rightarrow}
.
Note that because
c\in in(L)
, we have
c^{\prime}\in in(L)
by admissibility of
L
.

Now, for each
0\leq i\leq n
take
S^{\prime}_{i}=S_{i}\cap in(L)
and create the set
S^{\prime}=\{a\}\cup\bigcup\limits_{0\leq i\leq n}S^{\prime}_{i}
.
We have
S^{\prime C}\vDash_{C}\bigwedge S^{\prime C}
, therefore there exists a support
(S^{\prime},d)
for an argument
d
with
d^{C}=\bigwedge S^{\prime C}
.
Note that for all arguments
x\in S^{\prime}
, we have
x\in in(L)
, thus by admissibility of
L
we also have
d\in in(L)
.
Now consider again the attacker
c^{\prime}
:
As before, we can use
Sub(a^{\prime})^{C}\subseteq Sub(a)^{C}
to infer that, because
c^{\prime}
is gen-rebutting
b_{n}
,
c^{\prime}
is also gen-rebutting
d
.
Since
L(d)=\textit{IN}
, we can infer
L(c^{\prime})=\textit{OUT}
by admissibility of
L
.
Now
c
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L
and by admissibility of
L
we have
L(c)=\textit{OUT}
.
This contradicts our assumption
L(c)\neq\textit{OUT}
.
∎

5.3.3
Reduced versions of arguments

With the results from the previous sub-section, we are now equipped to consider the second key-insight mentioned in our proof overview:
Reduced verisons of arguments
.
We will show that – when considering preferred labelings – the labels of arguments and their reduced versions somewhat coincide.

Definition 15
.

Let
\mathcal{J}_{1}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{1}},{\preceq_{1}})
,
\mathcal{J}_{2}=(\mathcal{A}_{2},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{2}},{\preceq_{2}})
be two JSBAF which are syntactically disjoint.
Furthermore, let
\mathcal{J}_{+}=(\mathcal{A}_{+},{\rightarrow_{+}},{\Rightarrow_{+}},{\preceq_{+}})
be the union of
\mathcal{J}_{1}
and
\mathcal{J}_{2}
.
Lastly, let
a,a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{+}
be arguments s.t.
Atoms(a^{C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{i})
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
The argument
a^{\prime}
is a
reduced version
of
a
w.r.t.
AS_{i}
iff all of the following are satisfied:

•

a^{\prime C}=a^{C}

•

Atoms(a^{\prime})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{i})

•

For all
r\in R_{s}^{AX_{i}}\cup R_{d_{i}}
, there is
b\in Sub(a)
with
TR(b)=r
iff there is
b^{\prime}\in Sub(a^{\prime})
with
TR(b^{\prime})=r
.

•

If
b^{\prime}\in Sub(a^{\prime})
with
b^{\prime C}=\phi
, then there is
b\in Sub(a)
with
b^{C}=\phi
.

The following is clear from the definition of the atoms of an argument:

Corollary 3
.

Let
\mathcal{J}_{1}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{1}},{\preceq_{1}})
,
\mathcal{J}_{2}=(\mathcal{A}_{2},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{2}},{\preceq_{2}})
be two JSBAF which are syntactically disjoint.
Furthermore, let
\mathcal{J}_{+}=(\mathcal{A}_{+},{\rightarrow_{+}},{\Rightarrow_{+}},{\preceq_{+}})
be a union of
\mathcal{J}_{1}
and
\mathcal{J}_{2}
.
Lastly, let
a\in\mathcal{A}_{+}
be an argument s.t.
Atoms(a^{C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{i})
(
i\in\{1,2\}
).
If
a^{\prime}
is the reduced version of
a
w.r.t.
AS_{i}
, then
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
.

Next, we introduce a handy way to describe those arguments that we can reach by traversing the support-relation as far back as possible.
We will call these arguments
crucial sub-arguments
.

Definition 16
.

Let
AS_{1}=(R_{s_{1}},R_{d_{1}},n_{1},{\leq_{r_{1}}})
and
AS_{2}=(R_{s_{2}},R_{d_{2}},n_{2},{\leq_{r_{2}}})
be two AS s.t.
AS_{1}||AS_{2}
and let
AS_{+}=(R_{s}^{+},R_{d}^{+},n^{+},{\leq_{r}^{+}})
be a union of
AS_{1}
and
AS_{2}
.
Furthermore, let
\mathcal{J}_{1}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{1}},{\preceq_{1}})
,
\mathcal{J}_{2}=(\mathcal{A}_{2},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{2}},{\preceq_{2}})
and
\mathcal{J}_{+}=(\mathcal{A}_{+},{\rightarrow_{+}},{\Rightarrow_{+}},{\preceq_{+}})
be the JSBAFs corresponding to
AS_{1}
,
AS_{2}
and
AS_{+}
respectively.
The mapping
CSub:\mathcal{A}_{+}\rightarrow 2^{\mathcal{A}_{+}}
maps arguments to their
crucial sub-arguments
as follows:

•

If
TR(a)\in R_{s}^{AX_{+}}\cup R_{d_{+}}
, then
CSub(a)=\{a\}
.

•

If
TR(a)\in R_{s}^{\vDash}
, then we define:

\displaystyle CSub(a)=\big\{

\displaystyle a^{\prime}\in Sub(a)\mid TR(a^{\prime})\in R_{s}^{AX_{+}}\cup R_{d_{+}}

\displaystyle\textnormal{and }\mathcal{C}=\big\{(S_{0},b_{0}),\dots,(S_{m},a)\big\}\subseteq{\Rightarrow_{+}}

\displaystyle\textnormal{is a support chain with }a^{\prime}\in S_{0}\big\}

We define the
restriction
of
CSub
to
AS_{i}
(
i\in\{1,2\}
) as:

CSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}=\big\{a^{\prime}\in CSub(a)\mid Atoms(a^{\prime C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{i})\big\}
.

We will somewhat abuse the above notation for sets of arguments, i.e. for
S\subseteq\mathcal{A}(AS_{+})
we define
CSub(S)=\bigcup\limits_{a\in S}CSub(a)
.

By transitivity of
\vDash_{C}
and because the support-relation between arguments corresponds to the application of rules from
R_{s}^{\vDash}
, the following is easy to see:

Corollary 4
.

Let
AS=(R_{s},R_{d},n,\leq_{r})
be an AS.
For any
a\in\mathcal{A}(AS)
, we have
CSub(a)^{C}\vDash_{C}a^{C}
.

We will now show that, for a consistent argument
a
, there always exists a reduced verison
a^{\prime}
of
a
.
We start with the following auxilliary statement regarding the logical languages that we consider:

Proposition 18
.

Let
\Gamma=\{\phi_{0},...,\phi_{m},\phi\}
and
\Delta=\{\psi_{0},...,\psi_{n}\}
be syntactically disjoint sets of formulas.
Furthermore, let
\{\psi_{0},...\psi_{n}\}
be satisfiable.
If we have
\{\phi_{0},...,\phi_{m},\psi_{0},...,\psi_{n}\}\vDash_{C}\phi
, then
\{\phi_{0},...,\phi_{m}\}\vDash_{C}\phi
also holds.

Proof.

Towards a contradiction, assume that the claim does not hold.
Then there exists an interpretation
I_{1}
s.t.
I_{1}\vDash_{M}\phi_{i}
for all
\phi_{i}\in\{\phi_{0},...,\phi_{m}\}
but
I_{1}\not\vDash_{M}\phi
.
This means
I_{1}\vDash_{M}\lnot\phi
.
By assumption, the set
\{\psi_{0},...,\psi_{n}\}
is satisfiable, i.e. there exists an interpretation
I_{2}
s.t.
I_{2}\vDash_{M}\psi_{j}
for all of these
\psi_{j}
.
Because
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are syntactically disjoint, there now exists an interpretation
I
s.t.
I\vDash_{M}\phi_{i}
for all
\phi_{i}\in\{\phi_{0},...,\phi_{m}\}
,
I\vDash_{M}\psi_{j}
for all
\psi_{j}\in\{\psi_{0},...,\psi_{n}\}
and
I\vDash_{M}\lnot\phi
.
However, because
\{\phi_{0},...,\phi_{m},\psi_{0},...,\psi_{n}\}\vDash_{C}\phi
by assumption, we now also have
I\vDash_{M}\phi
, a contradiction to
I\vDash_{M}\lnot\phi
.
∎

Now for the existence of reduced versions of arguments:

Proposition 19
.

Let
\mathcal{J}_{1}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{1}},{\preceq_{1}})
,
\mathcal{J}_{2}=(\mathcal{A}_{2},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{2}},{\preceq_{2}})
be two JSBAF which are syntactically disjoint.
Furthermore, let
\mathcal{J}_{+}=(\mathcal{A}_{+},{\rightarrow_{+}},{\Rightarrow_{+}},{\preceq_{+}})
be a union of
\mathcal{J}_{1}
and
\mathcal{J}_{2}
.
Lastly, let
a\in\mathcal{A}_{+}
be a consistent argument s.t.
Atoms(a^{C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{i})
(
i\in\{1,2\}
).
Then there exists an argument
a^{\prime}
s.t.
a^{\prime}
is the reduced version of
a
w.r.t.
AS_{i}
.

Proof.

We show the claim by structured incution over the construction of
a
.
Induction start:
Let
a
be of the form
a:\rightsquigarrow\phi
.
By assumption,
Atoms(a^{C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{i})
.
Therefore,
a
itself is a reduced version of
a
w.r.t.
AS_{i}
.

Induction step:
Let
a
be of the form
a:a_{0},\dots,a_{m}\rightsquigarrow\phi
.
First, we make a distinction regarding the top-rule of
a
:
We disregard the case
TR(a)\in R_{s}^{AX_{+}}
, as it was covered by the induction start.
If
TR(a)\in R_{d}
, we can infer from
Atoms(a^{C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{i})
, that
TR(a)\in R_{d_{i}}
must hold.
Now for each
0\leq h\leq m
, we have
Atoms(a_{h}^{C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{i})
.
By the induction hypothesis, there exists reduced versions
a^{\prime}_{h}
(w.r.t.
AS_{i}
) for each of these arguments
a_{h}
.
Because
a_{h}^{C}=a_{h}^{\prime C}
, we can use these arguments to construct
a^{\prime}
as
a^{\prime}:a^{\prime}_{0},\dots a^{\prime}_{m}\Rightarrow\phi
.
It is clear that
a^{\prime}
satisfies the conditions of Definition
15
.

Now suppose that
TR(a)\in R_{s}^{\vDash}
holds and let
X=CSub(a)
.
Note that
X^{C}\vDash_{C}\phi
.
We partition the set
X
according to the top-rules of the arguments
x\in X
:
Let
X=X_{i}\cup X_{j}
with
X_{i}
containing precisely those
x
for which we have
TR(x)\in R_{s}^{AX_{i}}\cup R_{d_{i}}
and
X_{j}
containing precisely those
x
for which we have
TR(x)\in R_{s}^{AX_{j}}\cup R_{d_{j}}
.
Since
a
is consistent by assumption, the set
X_{j}^{C}
is satisfiable.
Since
Atoms(a)\subseteq Atoms(AS_{i})
by assumption, we can now use Proposition
18
to infer
X_{i}^{C}\vDash_{C}\phi
.

Now we construct a set of arguments
Y=\{y_{0},\dots,y_{l}\}
from
X_{i}\cup X_{j}
as follows:
If
x\in X_{i}
, then we add a reduced version of
x
w.r.t.
AS_{i}
to
Y
.
Such a reduced version exists by the induction hypothesis.
On the other hand, if
x\in X_{j}
, then we collect all arguments
z\in Sub(x)
for which we have
TR(z)\in R_{s}^{AX_{i}}\cup R_{d_{i}}
and add their reduced versions w.r.t.
AS_{i}
to
Y
.
Again, these reduced versions exist by the induction hypothesis.
We now claim that the argument
a^{\prime}:y_{0},\dots y_{l}\rightarrow\phi
exists and satisfies the conditions of Definition
15
.

We first argue for the existence of this argument.
For this, we have to show
Y^{C}\vDash_{C}\phi
.
Note that by construction of
Y
, we have
X_{i}^{C}\subseteq Y^{C}
.
As we have argued above,
X_{i}^{C}\vDash_{C}\phi
, thus monotonicity of
{\vDash_{C}}
yields
Y^{C}\vDash_{C}\phi
as required.

Next, we argue that
a^{\prime}
satisfies the conditions of Definition
15
:
We trivially have
a^{C}=a^{\prime C}
.
By construction of
Y
, we only added reduced arguments w.r.t.
AS_{i}
to
Y
, thus we have
Atoms(a^{\prime})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{i})
.
From the construction of
Y
, it is clear that for all
r\in R_{s}^{AX_{i}}\cup R_{d_{i}}
for which we have some
b^{\prime}\in Sub(a^{\prime})
with
TR(b^{\prime})=r
, there is some
b\in Sub(a)
with
TR(b)=r
.
Next, let
r\in R_{s}^{AX_{i}}\cup R_{d_{i}}
s.t. there is some
b\in Sub(a)
with
TR(b)=r
.
By construction of
X
, we can infer that
b\in Sub(x)
for some
x\in X_{i}
or some
x\in X_{j}
must hold.
In the first case, we added a reduced version of
x
to
Y
.
In the second case, we collected all
z\in Sub(x)
for which we have
TR(z)\in R_{s}^{AX_{i}}\cup R_{d_{i}}
and added their reduced versions
z^{\prime}
to
Y
.
In both cases, we can infer from the induction hypothesis, that there exists some
b^{\prime}\in Sub(a^{\prime})
with
TR(b^{\prime})=r
.
Lastly, because we only added reduced versions of arguments to
Y
and because
a^{\prime C}=a^{C}
, it is clear that if
b^{\prime}\in Sub(a^{\prime})
with
b^{\prime C}=\phi
, then there is some
b\in Sub(a)
s.t.
b^{C}=\phi
.
This finishes the proof.
∎

Now we are ready to show the main result regarding reduced versions of arguments:
For any argument
a
and its reduced version
a^{\prime}
, if
a
is accepted in a preferred labeling, then
a^{\prime}
is also accepted and if
a^{\prime}
is rejected, then
a
is also rejected.

Lemma 3
.

Let
\mathcal{J}_{1}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{1}},{\preceq_{1}})
,
\mathcal{J}_{2}=(\mathcal{A}_{2},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{2}},{\preceq_{2}})
be two JSBAF which are syntactically disjoint.
Furthermore, let
\mathcal{J}_{+}=(\mathcal{A}_{+},{\rightarrow_{+}},{\Rightarrow_{+}},{\preceq_{+}})
be the union of
\mathcal{J}_{1}
and
\mathcal{J}_{2}
.
Let
a\in\mathcal{A}_{+}
be a consistent argument s.t.
Atoms(a^{C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{i})
(
i\in\{1,2\}
) and let
a^{\prime}
be a reduced version of
a
w.r.t.
AS_{i}
.
If
L\in pr(\mathcal{J}_{+})
and
a
is legally IN w.r.t.
L
, then
L(a^{\prime})=\textit{IN}
.
If
L
is a labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{+}
s.t. for all
x\in\mathcal{A}_{+}
,
x\in out(L)
iff
x
is legally OUT w.r.t.
L
, then
a^{\prime}\in out(L)
implies
a\in out(L)
.

Proof.

We begin by showing that any attacker of
a^{\prime}
is also an attacker of
a
and that any support-chain
\mathcal{C^{\prime}}=\big\{(S^{\prime}_{0},b^{\prime}_{0}),\dots,(S^{\prime}_{n},b^{\prime}_{n})\big\}
with
a^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}_{0}
can be
“recreated”
with
a
instead of
a^{\prime}
.

First, assume that there is
(b,a^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
.
Then
b
either undercuts or gen-rebuts
a^{\prime}
.
If
b
undercuts
a^{\prime}
, then we can use
DR(a^{\prime})\subseteq DR(a)
to infer that
b
also undercuts
a
, meaning
(b,a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
.
If
b
gen-rebuts
a^{\prime}
, then we have
b^{C}=\lnot\bigwedge\Gamma
with
\Gamma\subseteq Sub(a^{\prime})^{C}\subseteq Sub(a)^{C}
.
Because
(b,a^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
, we have
b\not\prec_{ewl}a^{\prime}
, thus either
b\not\preceq_{ewl}a^{\prime}
or
a^{\prime}\preceq_{ewl}b
must hold.
If
b\not\preceq_{ewl}a^{\prime}
, then for all
r_{b}\in DR(b)
there exists
r_{a^{\prime}}\in DR(a^{\prime})\subseteq DR(a)
, s.t.
r_{b}\not\leq_{r}^{+}r_{a^{\prime}}
.
On the other hand, if
a^{\prime}\preceq_{ewl}b
, then there exists
r_{a^{\prime}}\in DR(a^{\prime})\subseteq DR(a)
s.t. for all
r_{b}\in DR(b)
,
r_{a^{\prime}}\leq_{r}^{+}r_{b}
.
In both cases, we can infer
b\not\prec_{ewl}a
and thus
(b,a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
, as required.

Next, assume that there is a support chain
\mathcal{C^{\prime}}=\big\{(S^{\prime}_{0},b^{\prime}_{0}),\dots,(S^{\prime}_{n},b^{\prime}_{n})\big\}
with
a^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}_{0}
.
Let
S^{\prime}_{0}=\{c_{0},\dots,a^{\prime},\dots,c_{m}\}
and
S_{0}=\{c_{0},\dots,a,\dots,c_{m}\}
.
We have
S_{0}^{\prime C}\vDash_{C}b_{0}^{\prime C}
and because
a^{\prime C}=a^{C}
, we must also have
S_{0}^{C}\vDash_{C}b_{0}^{\prime C}
.
Therefore, there exists the argument
b_{0}:c_{0},\dots,a,\dots,c_{m}\rightarrow b_{0}^{\prime C}
and the support
(S_{0},b_{0})
.
With the same reasoning, we can create a support chain
\mathcal{C}=\big\{(S_{0},b_{0}),\dots,(S_{n},b_{n})\big\}
with
a\in S_{0}
,
b_{k}^{\prime C}=b_{k}^{C}
and
S_{k}=(S_{k}^{\prime}\setminus\{b_{k-1}^{\prime}\})\cup\{b_{k-1}\}
for all
0<k\leq n
.
Now we can use
DR(a^{\prime})\subseteq DR(a)
to infer the following:
If
d\in S_{0}^{\prime}\setminus\{a^{\prime}\}
s.t.
a^{\prime}\preceq_{ewl}d
, then there is some
r_{a}\in DR(a^{\prime})\subseteq DR(a)
s.t. for all
r_{d}\in DR(d)
, we have
r_{a}\leq_{r}^{+}r_{d}
.
This, in turn, implies we also have
a\preceq_{ewl}d
.
With the same reasoning, for any
0<k\leq n
, if we have
d\in S_{k}^{\prime}\setminus\{b_{k-1}^{\prime}\}
s.t.
b_{k-1}^{\prime}\preceq_{ewl}d
, then
b_{k-1}\preceq_{ewl}d
also holds.
Furthermore, because
a^{\prime}
is a reduced version of
a
by assumption, we have
DR(b_{n}^{\prime})\subseteq DR(b_{n})
and
Sub(b_{n}^{\prime})^{C}\subseteq Sub(b_{n})^{C}
.
Now, let
(d,b_{n}^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
be an attack towards
b_{n}^{\prime}
.
Similar to the case
(b,a^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
above, we can infer that
(d,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
must also hold:
If
d
undercuts
b_{n}^{\prime}
, then it must do so on an argument
x\in CSub(b_{n}^{\prime})
and because
a^{\prime}
is a reduced version of
a
, we can infer that
d
also undercuts
b_{n}
, meaning
(d,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
.
On the other hand, if
d
gen-rebuts
b_{n}^{\prime}
, then
d^{C}=\lnot\bigwedge\Gamma
and
d\not\prec_{ewl}b_{n}^{\prime}
, for
\Gamma\subseteq Sub(b_{n}^{\prime})^{C}\subseteq Sub(b_{n})^{C}
.
Analogous to before, we can show that
d\not\prec_{ewl}b_{n}
also holds, meaning we again have
(d,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
.
Therefore, we can essentially
“recreate”
the support chains
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
starting at
a^{\prime}
as support chains
\mathcal{C}
which start at
a
.

Now for the actual claim:
Suppose first that
L
is a labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{+}
for which we have that,
x\in out(L)
iff
x
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L
.
Assume that
a^{\prime}
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L
.
Then this must be due to an attack or a support.
If there is an attack
(d,a^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
with
d\in in(L)
, then we have argued above that
(d,a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
also holds, therefore
a
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L
.
On the other hand, if there is a support chain
\mathcal{C^{\prime}}=\big\{(S_{0}^{\prime},b_{0}^{\prime}),\dots,(S_{n}^{\prime},b_{n}^{\prime})\big\}
s.t.
a^{\prime}\in S_{0}^{\prime}
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L
, then we have argued above that there exists a support chain
\mathcal{C}=\big\{(S_{0},b_{0}),\dots,(S_{n},b_{n})\big\}
with
a\in S_{0}
.
Note that in the support chain
\mathcal{C}
, all arguments in the supporting sets
S_{k}
are the same as in the supporting sets
S_{k}^{\prime}
in the support chain
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, except for the newly created arguments
b_{k}
.
More precisely, we have
S_{0}^{\prime}\setminus\{a^{\prime}\}=S_{0}\setminus\{a\}
and for each
0<k\leq n
we have
S_{k}^{\prime}\setminus\{b_{k-1}^{\prime}\}=S_{k}\setminus\{b_{k-1}\}
.

Since
a^{\prime}
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L
by assumption, we have
L(b_{l}^{\prime})=\textit{OUT}
for each
0\leq l\leq n
.
Now we can make an inductive argument to show that
L(b_{l})=\textit{OUT}
also holds:
For the induction start, we note that
L(b_{n}^{\prime})=\textit{OUT}
due to an attack
(c,b_{n}^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
with
L(c)=\textit{IN}
.
We have argued above that this means
(c,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
also holds, therefore
b_{n}
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L
and by our assumption this implies
L(b_{n})=\textit{OUT}
.
For the induction step, we first note that, since each
b_{k}^{\prime}\in\{b_{0}^{\prime},\dots,b_{n-1}^{\prime}\}
is labeled
OUT
, they must be legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L
by our assumption.
From this we can infer that
b_{k}^{\prime}\preceq d
for all
d\in S_{k+1}^{\prime}\setminus\{b_{k}^{\prime}\}
and by the way JSBAFs are constructed from their AS counterparts, we can infer
b_{k}^{\prime}\preceq_{ewl}d
.
As we have argued above, this implies
b_{k}\preceq_{ewl}d
for all
d\in S_{k+1}\setminus\{b_{k}^{\prime}\}
, therefore
b_{k}\preceq d
also holds for these arguments
d
.
Since
S_{k+1}^{\prime}\setminus\{b_{k}^{\prime}\}=S_{k+1}\setminus\{b_{k}\}
, we now have that each
b_{k}
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L
and therefore
L(b_{k})=\textit{OUT}
also holds.
In particular, this means
L(b_{0})\in\textit{OUT}
.
Since
S_{0}^{\prime}\setminus\{a^{\prime}\}=S_{0}\setminus\{a\}
and since
a^{\prime}\preceq d
for all
d\in S_{0}^{\prime}\setminus\{a^{\prime}\}
, we can now also infer that
a
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L
, as required.

Now suppose that
L\in pr(\mathcal{J}_{+})
and that
a
is legally
IN
w.r.t.
L
.
We will show that for the reduced argument
a^{\prime}
, the prerequisites for Proposition
17
are satisfied.
If this holds, then we can use Proposition
17
to infer that
L_{a^{\prime}}
is an admissible labeling.
Since
L
is a preferred labeling, we can then infer that
L(a^{\prime})=\textit{IN}
holds as required, since
L(a^{\prime})\neq\textit{IN}
would contradict the maximality (w.r.t. set-inclusion of accepted arguments) of
L
.
Thus we only need to show that the prerequisites for Proposition
17
are satisfied.

First of all, we can use the above proof to infer that
L(a^{\prime})\neq\textit{OUT}
must hold:
If
L(a^{\prime})=\textit{OUT}
, then by admissibility of
L
,
a^{\prime}
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L
.
By our argumentation above, this implies
a
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L
, contradicting
a\in in(L)
.
Next, for the attackers of
a^{\prime}
:
Towards a contradiction, suppose that there is an attack
(d,a^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow}
with
L(d)\neq\textit{OUT}
.
We have argued above that this implies
(d,a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
.
Now
a
is not legally
IN
w.r.t.
L
, a contradiction.
Lastly, for the support chains starting at
a^{\prime}
:
Towards a contradiction, assume that there is a support chain
\mathcal{C^{\prime}}=\big\{(S_{0}^{\prime},b_{0}^{\prime}),\dots,(S_{n}^{\prime},b_{n}^{\prime})\big\}
and an attack
(d,b_{n}^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
with
a^{\prime}\in S_{0}^{\prime}
,
L(d)\neq\textit{OUT}
,
S_{0}^{\prime}\setminus\{a^{\prime}\}\subseteq in(L)
and
S_{k}^{\prime}\setminus\{b_{k-1}^{\prime}\}\subseteq in(L)
for all
0<k\leq n
.
As we have argued above, there now also exists a support chain
\mathcal{C}=\big\{(S_{0},b_{0}),\dots,(S_{n},b_{n})\big\}
and an attack
(d,b_{n})
with
a\in S_{0}
,
L(d)\neq\textit{OUT}
,
S_{0}\setminus\{a\}\subseteq in(L)
and
S_{k}\setminus\{b_{k-1}\}\subseteq in(L)
for all
0<k\leq n
.
Now none of the
b_{k}\in\{b_{0},\dots,b_{n}\}
are legally
IN
w.r.t.
L
, and by admissiblity of
L
we can infer that none of them are actually labeled
IN
.
For the support
(S_{0},b_{0})
we now have
L(b_{0})\neq\textit{IN}
but
S_{0}\subseteq in(L)
, which means none of the arguments in
S_{0}
are legally
IN
w.r.t.
L
.
This contradicts the admissibility of
L
and finishes the proof.
∎

5.3.4
Interactions between argumentation systems

In this section, we will consider the first key insight mentioned in our proof overview: The four
“edge cases”
depicted in Illustration
1
.
We start with an auxiliary proposition which tells us that, if the conclusion of an argument
a\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
contains only atoms of
AS_{j}
, then either
a
is inconsistent or its conclusion is a tautology.

Proposition 20
.

Let
AS_{1}=(R_{s_{1}},R_{d_{1}},n_{1},{\leq_{r_{1}}})
and
AS_{2}=(R_{s_{2}},R_{d_{2}},n_{2},{\leq_{r_{2}}})
be two AS s.t.
AS_{1}||AS_{2}
.
Furthermore, let
a\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
be an argument with
TR(a)\in R_{s}^{\vDash}
.
If
Atoms(a^{C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{j})
, then either
a
is inconsistent or
\phi
is a tautology.

Proof.

Let
a
be of the form
a:a_{0},\dots,a_{m}\rightarrow\phi
.
Towards a contradiction, suppose that the claim does not hold, meaning we have
Atoms(\phi)\subseteq Atoms(AS_{j})
,
a\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
is consistent and
\phi
is not a tautology.
Let
\Gamma=ADSub(a)^{C}
.
By Corollary
2
, we know that
\Gamma\vDash_{C}\phi
.
Because
a\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
, we know that for each
\psi\in\Gamma
, we have
Atoms(\psi)\subseteq Atoms(AS_{i})
.
By
AS_{1}||AS_{2}
, we can now infer that
\phi
and
\Gamma
must be syntactically disjoint.
Since
a
is consistent by assumption, there is an interpretation
I_{1}
s.t.
I_{1}\vDash_{M}\psi
for each
\psi\in\Gamma
.
Because we also assumed that
\phi
is not a tautology, there exists an interpretation
I_{2}
s.t.
I_{2}\vDash_{M}-\phi
.
Because
\phi
and
\Gamma
are disjoint, there now exists an interpretation
I
s.t.
I\vDash_{M}\psi
for each
\psi\in\Gamma
, but
I\vDash_{M}-\phi
also holds.
This contradicts
\Gamma\vDash_{C}\phi
, therefore the claim must hold.
∎

Now we are ready to consider the first case of Illustration
1
.
Using the proposition above, we will show that, for any syntactically disjoint
AS_{1}
and
AS_{2}
as well as their union
AS_{+}
, if an argument
a\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
attacks an argument
b\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
, then either
a
or
b
is rejected by any admissible labeling of the corresponding JSBAFs.

Proposition 21
.

Let
AS_{1}=(R_{s_{1}},R_{d_{1}},n_{1},{\leq_{r_{1}}})
,
AS_{2}=(R_{s_{2}},R_{d_{2}},n_{2},{\leq_{r_{2}}})
be two
AS
s.t.
AS_{1}||AS_{2}
and let
AS_{+}
be their union.
Furthermore, let
\mathcal{J}_{1}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{1}},{\preceq_{1}})
,
\mathcal{J}_{2}=(\mathcal{A}_{2},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{2}},{\preceq_{2}})
and
\mathcal{J}_{+}=(\mathcal{A}_{+},{\rightarrow_{+}},{\Rightarrow_{+}},{\preceq_{+}})
be the JSBAFs corresponding to
AS_{1}
,
AS_{2}
and
AS_{+}
.
Lastly, for
i,j\in\{1,2\}
with
i\neq j
, let
a\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
b\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
be two arguments.
If
(a,b)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
, then there exists
c\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\cap\mathcal{A}_{j}\cap\mathcal{A}_{+}
s.t.
c
is of the form
c:\rightarrow\phi
,
TR(c)\in R_{s}^{\vDash}
and either
(c,a)\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
or
(c,b)\in{\rightarrow_{j}}
.

Proof.

By
(a,b)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
, we know that
a
is either undercutting or gen-rebutting
b
.
We first show the undercut case.

W.l.o.g. , we assume that
a
undercuts
b
on
b
itself.
Let
a
and
b
be of the form
a:a_{0},\dots,a_{m}\rightsquigarrow\phi
and
b:b_{0},\dots,b_{k}\Rightarrow\phi
with
TR(b)=r\in R_{d_{j}}
and
-\phi=n_{j}(r)
.
Because
TR(b)\in R_{d_{j}}
, we have
Atoms(\phi)\subseteq Atoms(AS_{j})
.
Thus we know that
TR(a)\in R_{s}^{\vDash}
must hold, otherwise the syntactic disjointness of
AS_{1}
and
AS_{2}
would be violated.
By Proposition
20
, we can now infer that either
\phi
is a tautology or
a
is inconsistent.
If
a
is inconsistent, then there is
\Gamma\subseteq Sub(a)^{C}
s.t.
\Gamma
is unsatisfiable.
Now
\lnot\bigwedge\Gamma
is a tautology and there exists an argument
\overline{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\cap\mathcal{A}_{j}\cap\mathcal{A}_{+}
of the form
\overline{a}:\rightarrow\lnot\bigwedge\Gamma
which gen-rebuts
a
.
Since
\overline{a}
is a strict argument, we have
\overline{a}\not\prec a
, therefore
(\overline{a},a)\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
, as claimed.
Next, assume that
a
is consistent.
Then
\phi
must be a tautology.
This means there exists an argument
\overline{b}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\cap\mathcal{A}_{j}\cap\mathcal{A}_{+}
of the form
\overline{b}:\rightarrow\phi
which undercuts
b
, meaning
(\overline{b},b)\in{\rightarrow}_{j}
.
This concludes the undercut case.

Now for the gen-rebut case:
If
a
gen-rebuts
b
, then
b
is defeasible and there is
\Gamma\subseteq Sub(b)^{C}
s.t.
a^{C}=\phi=\lnot\bigwedge\Gamma
.
We make a distinction w.r.t.
TR(a)
.

In the first case, assume that
TR(a)\in R_{d_{i}}\cup R_{s}^{AX_{i}}
.
Then for all
\psi\in\Gamma
, we have
Atoms(\psi)\subseteq Atoms(AS_{i})
.
Thus for all
b^{\prime}\in Sub(b)
with
b^{\prime C}=\psi^{\prime}\in\Gamma
, we must have
TR(b^{\prime})\in R_{s}^{\vDash}
, otherwise syntactic disjointness of
AS_{1}
and
AS_{2}
would be violated again.
Now we can use Proposition
20
again to infer that for each of these sub-arguments of
b
, either
b^{\prime}
is inconsistent or
\psi^{\prime}
a tautology.
If any of the
b^{\prime}
are inconsistent, then
b
is also inconsistent and we can construct the argument
\overline{b}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\cap\mathcal{A}_{j}\cap\mathcal{A}_{+}
as before.
We therefore assume that all
b^{\prime}
are consistent.
But then all
\psi^{\prime}\in\Gamma
are tautologies, which means
\bigwedge\Gamma
is also a tautology.
Now
\lnot\bigwedge\Gamma=a^{C}
is unsatisfiable, thus
a
is inconsistent and we can construct the argument
\overline{a}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\cap\mathcal{A}_{j}\cap\mathcal{A}_{+}
as before.

Now for the second case, i.e.
TR(a)\in R_{s}^{\vDash}
.
Let
\Delta_{a}=ADSub(a)^{C}
and
\Delta_{b}=ADSub(b)^{C}
be the conclusions of the axiomatic and defeasible sub-arguments of
a
and
b
respectively.
By Proposition
10
, we have
\Delta_{a}\vDash_{C}\lnot\bigwedge\Gamma
and
\Delta_{b}\vDash_{C}\bigwedge\Gamma
.
Because
AS_{1}
and
AS_{2}
are syntactically disjoint,
\Delta_{a}
and
\Delta_{b}
must also be syntactically disjoint.
Now, towards a contradiction, assume that
a
and
b
are both consistent.
Then in particular
\Delta_{a}
and
\Delta_{b}
are satisfiable, i.e. there exists interpretations
I_{a},I_{b}
s.t.
I_{a}\vDash_{M}\phi_{a}
for all
\phi_{a}\in\Delta_{a}
and
I_{b}\vDash_{M}\phi_{b}
for all
\phi_{b}\in\Delta_{b}
.
Because
\Delta_{a}
and
\Delta_{b}
are syntactically disjoint, there now exists an interpretation
I
s.t.
I\vDash_{M}\phi
for all
\phi\in\Delta_{a}\cup\Delta_{b}
.
Because we have
\Delta_{a}\vDash_{C}\lnot\bigwedge\Gamma
and
\Delta_{b}\vDash_{C}\bigwedge\Gamma
, we can now infer that
I\vDash_{M}\lnot\bigwedge\Gamma
and
I\vDash_{M}\bigwedge\Gamma
must hold, a contradiction.
We conclude that either
a
or
b
be need to be inconsistent and we can again construct one of the arguments
\overline{a}
,
\overline{b}
as before.
∎

Before we continue with our next results, we need one more definition, which will be used to adequately describe those arguments that correspond to the argument
b
in Illustration
1
.
More precisely, we use the term
minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
-argument
for those arguments
b\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
, which have crucial sub-arguments in
\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
\mathcal{A}_{j}
, while not having any crucial sub-arguments in
\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
.

Definition 17
.

Let
AS_{1}=(R_{s_{1}},R_{d_{1}},n_{1},{\leq_{r_{1}}})
,
AS_{2}=(R_{s_{2}},R_{d_{2}},n_{2},{\leq_{r_{2}}})
be two
AS
s.t.
AS_{1}||AS_{2}
and let
AS_{+}
be their union.
Furthermore, let
a\in\mathcal{A}_{+}
be an argument.
We say that
a
is a
minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
-argument
, iff
CSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\neq\emptyset
,
CSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}\neq\emptyset
and
CSub(a)\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j}
.

Now we prove three propositions which, respectively, cover cases two, three and four of Illustration
1
.
For now, we only show that if these cases exist, then we can infer the existence of arguments and attacks which are wholly contained in either
\mathcal{J}_{1}
or
\mathcal{J}_{2}
.
Afterwards, we will combine all these cases to infer that a preferred labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{i}
can be
“extended”
to a preferred labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{+}
and a preferred labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{+}
can be
“reduced”
to a preferred labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{i}
.

Proposition 22
.

Let
AS_{1}=(R_{s_{1}},R_{d_{1}},n_{1},{\leq_{r_{1}}})
,
AS_{2}=(R_{s_{2}},R_{d_{2}},n_{2},{\leq_{r_{2}}})
be two
AS
s.t.
AS_{1}||AS_{2}
and let
AS_{+}
be their union.
Let
\mathcal{J}_{1}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{1}},{\preceq_{1}})
,
\mathcal{J}_{2}=(\mathcal{A}_{2},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{2}},{\preceq_{2}})
and
\mathcal{J}_{+}=(\mathcal{A}_{+},{\rightarrow_{+}},{\Rightarrow_{+}},{\preceq_{+}})
be the JSBAFs corresponding to
AS_{1}
,
AS_{2}
and
AS_{+}
respectively.
Furthermore, let
a,b\in\mathcal{A}_{+}
be arguments s.t.
a
is a consistent minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
-argument.
Lastly, let
(a,b)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
.
If
b\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
, then there exists an argument
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
s.t.
(a^{\prime},b)\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
and
CSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}=CSub(a^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Throughout this proof, let
\Gamma_{i}=CSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
and
\Gamma_{j}=CSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}
.
Note that this means
\Gamma_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
\Gamma_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{j}
because
a
is a minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
-argument.
First, assume that
(a,b)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
is the result of an undercut.
Then we know
Atoms(a^{C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{i})
.
Furthermore,
Atoms(\Gamma_{i}^{C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{i})
and
Atoms(\Gamma_{j}^{C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{j})
, i.e.
\Gamma_{i}^{C}\cup\{a^{C}\}
and
\Gamma_{j}^{C}
are syntactically disjoint sets of formulas.
By assumption, we also know that
\Gamma_{i}^{C}\cup\{a^{C}\}
as well as
\Gamma_{j}^{C}
are consistent sets of formulas.
Now we can use Proposition
18
to infer
\Gamma_{i}^{C}\vDash_{C}a^{C}
.
Thus we can construct the argument
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
as follows:

a^{\prime}:a_{0},\dots,a_{m}\rightarrow a^{C}
, where
\{a_{0},...,a_{m}\}=\Gamma_{i}
.
Clearly,
a^{\prime}
undercuts
b
, therefore
(a^{\prime},b)\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
as required.

Next, assume that
(a,b)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
is the result of a gen-rebut.
Let
\Delta=\{\phi_{0},...,\phi_{m}\}
and
a^{C}=\lnot\bigwedge\Delta
.
By Proposition
10
, we know that
ADSub(b)^{C}\vDash_{C}\bigwedge\Delta
.
We claim that
\Gamma_{i}^{C}\vDash_{C}\lnot\bigwedge ADSub(b)^{C}
also holds.
Towards a contradiction, assume that this is not the case.
Then there exists an interpretation
I_{1}
s.t.
I_{1}\vDash_{M}\Gamma_{i}^{C}
and
I_{1}\vDash_{M}\bigwedge ADSub(b)^{C}
.
By assumption
a
is consistent, thus there also exists an interpretation
I_{2}
s.t.
I_{2}\vDash_{M}\Gamma_{j}^{C}
.
Note that
Atoms(ADSub(b)^{C})\cup Atoms(\Gamma_{i}^{C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{i})
since
b\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
, while
Atoms(\Gamma_{j}^{C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{j})
.
Now there exists an interpretation
I
s.t.
I\vDash_{M}\Gamma_{i}^{C}\cup\Gamma_{j}^{C}
, i.e.
I\vDash_{M}\lnot\bigwedge\Delta
and
I\vDash_{M}ADSub(b)^{C}
, i.e.
I\vDash_{M}\bigwedge\Delta
.
Obviously, this is a contradiction.
We infer
\Gamma_{i}^{C}\vDash_{C}\lnot\bigwedge ADSub(b)^{C}
.

With this, we can construct the argument
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
as follows:

a^{\prime}:a_{0},\dots,a_{m}\rightarrow\lnot\bigwedge ADSub(b)^{C}
, where
\{a_{0},\dots,a_{m}\}=\Gamma_{i}
.
Because
(a,b)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
, we know that
a\not\prec b
.
Thus we must have either
a\not\preceq b
or
b\preceq a
.
In the first case, we can infer that for all
r_{a}\in DR(a)\supseteq DR(a^{\prime})
, there exists
r_{b}\in DR(b)
s.t.
r_{a}\not\leq r_{b}
.
In the second case, we can infer that there exists
r_{b}\in DR(b)
s.t. for all
r_{a}\in DR(a)\supseteq DR(a^{\prime})
, we have
r_{b}\leq r_{a}
.
In both cases we can infer
a^{\prime}\not\prec b
, therefore
(a^{\prime},b)\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
as required.
∎

Proposition 23
.

Let
AS_{1}=(R_{s_{1}},R_{d_{1}},n_{1},{\leq_{r_{1}}})
,
AS_{2}=(R_{s_{2}},R_{d_{2}},n_{2},{\leq_{r_{2}}})
be two
AS
s.t.
AS_{1}||AS_{2}
and let
AS_{+}
be their union.
Let
\mathcal{J}_{1}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{1}},{\preceq_{1}})
,
\mathcal{J}_{2}=(\mathcal{A}_{2},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{2}},{\preceq_{2}})
and
\mathcal{J}_{+}=(\mathcal{A}_{+},{\rightarrow_{+}},{\Rightarrow_{+}},{\preceq_{+}})
be the JSBAFs corresponding to
AS_{1}
,
AS_{2}
and
AS_{+}
respectively.
Furthermore, let
a,b\in\mathcal{A}_{+}
be consistent arguments, s.t.
a\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
b
is a minimal
AS_{+}
-argument (
i\in\{1,2\}
).
Lastly, let
(a,b)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
.
Then either there exist arguments
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
, s.t.
CSub(a^{\prime})=CSub(a)
,
CSub(b^{\prime})=CSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
and
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
, or there exists arguments
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
s.t.
CSub(a^{\prime})=CSub(a)
,
CSub(b^{\prime})=ADSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
and either
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
or
(b^{\prime},a^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
.

Proof.

First, assume that
(a,b)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
is the result of an undercut.
Then this undercut must be directed towards an argument in
CSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
or in
CSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}
.
In the second case, we have
Atoms(a^{C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{j})
.
With the same argumentation as in the proof of Proposition
21
, we can show that this implies either
a
or
b
need to be inconsistent.
Because this contradicts our assumptions, we infer that, if
a
undercuts
b
, then it must do so on some
b^{\prime}\in CSub(B)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
.
Now, for
CSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}=\{b_{0},...,b_{m}\}
, let
b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
be of the form
b^{\prime}:b_{0},...,b_{m}\rightarrow\bigwedge\{b_{0},...,b_{m}\}^{C}
.
Obviously we have
CSub(b^{\prime})=CSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
.
Now
a
undercuts
b^{\prime}
, meaning
(a,b^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
as required.

Now for the gen-rebut case:
Let
a^{C}=\lnot\bigwedge\Gamma
.
We claim that
CSub(a)^{C}\vDash_{C}\lnot\bigwedge\big(ADSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\big)^{C}
.
Towards a contradiction, assume that this is not true.
Then there exists an interpretation
I_{1}
s.t.
I_{1}\vDash_{M}CSub(a)^{C}
and
I_{1}\vDash_{M}\bigwedge\big(ADSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\big)^{C}
.
Because
b
was consistent by assumption, there also exists an interpretation
I_{2}
for which we have
I_{2}\vDash_{M}\big(ADSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}\big)^{C}
.
Note that
CSub(a)^{C}\cup\big(ADSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\big)^{C}
and
\big(ADSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}\big)^{C}
are syntactically disjoint sets of formulas.
Now there exists an interpretation
I
for which we have both
I\vDash_{M}CSub(a)^{C}
and
I\vDash_{M}\big(ADSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\big)^{C}\cup\big(ADSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}\big)^{C}
, i.e.
I\vDash_{M}ADSub(b)^{C}
.
By Corollary
4
we can infer that
I\vDash_{M}\lnot\bigwedge\Gamma
and by Proposition
10
we can infer
I\vDash_{M}\bigwedge\Gamma
.
Obviously, this is a contradiction, therefore
CSub(a)^{C}\vDash_{C}\lnot\bigwedge\big(ADSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\big)^{C}
must hold.

Now we construct the arguments
a^{\prime},b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
as follows:
For
CSub(a)=\{a_{0},\dots,a_{m}\}
, let
a^{\prime}:a_{0},\dots,a_{m}\rightarrow\lnot\bigwedge\big(ADSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\big)^{C}
, and for
ADSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}=\{b_{0},\dots,b_{n}\}
, let
b^{\prime}:b_{0},\dots,b_{n}\rightarrow\bigwedge\{b_{0},\dots,b_{n}\}^{C}
and
b^{\prime\prime}:b^{\prime}\rightarrow\lnot\lnot\bigwedge\{b_{0},\dots,b_{n}\}^{C}
.
Clearly,
a^{\prime}
gen-rebuts
b^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime\prime}
gen-rebuts
a^{\prime}
.
Now, if
a^{\prime}\not\prec b^{\prime}
, then
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
and the claim holds.
Thus we assume that
a^{\prime}\prec b^{\prime}
.
Because
DR(b^{\prime})=DR(b^{\prime\prime})
, this also implies
a^{\prime}\prec b^{\prime\prime}
, i.e.
a^{\prime}\preceq b^{\prime\prime}
and
b^{\prime\prime}\not\preceq a^{\prime}
.
From this we infer
b^{\prime\prime}\not\prec a^{\prime}
, therefore
(b^{\prime\prime},a^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
as required.
∎

Proposition 24
.

Let
AS_{1}=(R_{s_{1}},R_{d_{1}},n_{1},{\leq_{r_{1}}})
,
AS_{2}=(R_{s_{2}},R_{d_{2}},n_{2},{\leq_{r_{2}}})
be two
AS
s.t.
AS_{1}||AS_{2}
and let
AS_{+}
be their union.
Let
\mathcal{J}_{1}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{1}},{\preceq_{1}})
,
\mathcal{J}_{2}=(\mathcal{A}_{2},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{2}},{\preceq_{2}})
and
\mathcal{J}_{+}=(\mathcal{A}_{+},{\rightarrow_{+}},{\Rightarrow_{+}},{\preceq_{+}})
be the JSBAFs corresponding to
AS_{1}
,
AS_{2}
and
AS_{+}
respectively.
Furthermore, let
a,b\in\mathcal{A}_{+}
be arguments, s.t. both
a
and
b
are consistent minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
-arguments.
Lastly, let
(a,b)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
.
Then one of the following four cases holds:

1.

There are
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
s.t.
CSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}=CSub(a^{\prime})
,
CSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}=CSub(b^{\prime})
and
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
.

2.

There are
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
s.t.
CSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}=CSub(a^{\prime})
,
CSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}=CSub(b^{\prime})
and
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{j}}
.

3.

There are
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
s.t.
CSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}=CSub(a^{\prime})
,
ADSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}=CSub(b^{\prime})
and either
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
or
(b^{\prime},a^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
.

4.

There are
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
s.t.
CSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}=CSub(a^{\prime})
,
ADSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}=CSub(b^{\prime})
and either
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{j}}
or
(b^{\prime},a^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{j}}
.

Proof.

Throughout this proof, let
\Gamma_{a_{i}}=CSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
,
\Gamma_{a_{j}}=CSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}
and let
\Gamma_{b_{i}}=CSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
,
\Gamma_{b_{j}}=CSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}
.
First, assume that
(a,b)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
is the result of an undercut.
Then either
Atoms(a^{C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{i})
or
Atoms(a^{C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{j})
.
Because
CSub(b)=CSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\cup CSub(b)\!\!\mid_{AS_{j}}
, we know that this undercut needs to be directed towards an argument which is either in
\Gamma_{b_{i}}
or in
\Gamma_{b_{j}}
.
Note that we have
\Gamma_{b_{i}}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
\Gamma_{b_{j}}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{j}
.
Now we can use Proposition
18
to infer that, if
Atoms(a^{C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{i})
, then
\Gamma_{a_{i}}^{C}\vDash_{C}a^{C}
and if
Atoms(a^{C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{j})
, then
\Gamma_{a_{j}}^{C}\vDash_{C}a^{C}
.
In the first case, we can construct the arguments
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
which are of the form
a^{\prime}:a_{0},\dots,a_{m}\rightarrow a^{C}
and
b^{\prime}:b_{0},\dots,b_{n}\rightarrow\bigwedge\{b_{0},\dots,b_{n}\}^{C}
, where
\{a_{0},\dots,a_{m}\}=\Gamma_{a_{i}}
and
\{b_{0},\dots,b_{n}\}=\Gamma_{b_{i}}
.
Then
CSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}=CSub(a^{\prime})
,
CSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}=CSub(b^{\prime})
and
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
, as required by item one of the claim.
In the second case, we can construct the arguments
a^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
which are of the form
a^{\prime\prime}:a_{0},\dots,a_{m}\rightarrow a^{C}
and
b^{\prime\prime}:b_{0},\dots,b_{n}\rightarrow\bigwedge\{b_{0},...,b_{n}\}^{C}
, where
\{a_{0},\dots,a_{m}\}=\Gamma_{a_{j}}
and
\{b_{0},\dots,b_{n}\}=\Gamma_{b_{j}}
.
Then
CSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}=CSub(a^{\prime\prime})
,
CSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}=CSub(b^{\prime\prime})
and
(a^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{j}}
, as required by item two of the claim.

Now for the gen-rebut case.
Let
\Delta=\{\phi_{0},...,\phi_{m}\}
and
a^{C}=\lnot\bigwedge\Delta
.
We claim that either
\Gamma_{a_{i}}^{C}\vDash_{C}\lnot\bigwedge\big(ADSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\big)^{C}
or
\Gamma_{a_{j}}^{C}\vDash_{C}\lnot\bigwedge\big(ADSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}\big)^{C}
must hold.
Towards a contradiction, assume that this is not the case.
Then there exists two interpretations,
I_{1},I_{2}
s.t.
I_{1}\vDash_{M}\Gamma_{a_{i}}^{C}
,
I_{1}\vDash_{M}\bigwedge\big(ADSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\big)^{C}
and
I_{2}\vDash_{M}\Gamma_{a_{j}}^{C}
,
I_{2}\vDash_{M}\bigwedge\big(ADSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}\big)^{C}
.
Note that
\Gamma_{a_{i}}^{C}\cup\big(ADSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\big)^{C}
and
\Gamma_{a_{j}}^{C}\cup\big(ADSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}\big)^{C}
are syntactically disjoint sets of formulas.
Now there exists an interpretation
I
s.t.
I\vDash_{M}\Gamma_{a_{i}}\cup\Gamma_{a_{j}}
, i.e.
I\vDash_{M}\lnot\bigwedge\Delta
and
I\vDash_{M}\big(ADSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\big)^{C}\cup\big(ADSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}\big)^{C}
, i.e.
I\vDash_{M}\bigwedge\Delta
.
Obviously, this is a contradiction.
We infer that either
\Gamma_{a_{i}}^{C}\vDash_{C}\lnot\bigwedge\big(ADSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\big)^{C}
or
\Gamma_{a_{j}}^{C}\vDash_{C}\lnot\bigwedge\big(ADSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}\big)^{C}
must hold.

In the first case, we can construct the arguments
a^{\prime},b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
which are of the form
a^{\prime}:a_{0},\dots,a_{m}\rightarrow\lnot\bigwedge\{b_{0},\dots,b_{n}\}^{C}
,
b^{\prime}:b_{0},\dots,b_{n}\rightarrow\bigwedge\{b_{0},\dots,b_{n}\}^{C}
and
b^{\prime\prime}:b^{\prime}\rightarrow\lnot\lnot\bigwedge\{b_{0},\dots,b_{n}\}^{C}
, where
\{a_{0},\dots,a_{m}\}=\Gamma_{a_{i}}
and
\{b_{0},\dots,b_{n}\}=ADSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
.
Then
a^{\prime}
gen-rebuts
b^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime\prime}
gen-rebuts
a^{\prime}
.
Analogous to the proof of the gen-rebut case in Proposition
22
, we either have
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
(if
a^{\prime}\not\prec b^{\prime}
), or we have
(b^{\prime\prime},a^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
(if
a^{\prime}\prec b^{\prime}
).
Either way, item three of the claim is satisfied.

In the second case, we can construct the arguments
a^{\prime},b^{\prime},b^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
which are of the form
a^{\prime}:a_{0},\dots,a_{m}\rightarrow\lnot\bigwedge\{b_{0},\dots,b_{n}\}^{C}
,
b^{\prime}:b_{0},\dots,b_{n}\rightarrow\bigwedge\{b_{0},\dots,b_{n}\}^{C}
and
b^{\prime\prime}:b^{\prime}\rightarrow\lnot\lnot\bigwedge\{b_{0},\dots,b_{n}\}^{C}
, where
\{a_{0},\dots,a_{m}\}=\Gamma_{a_{j}}
and
\{b_{0},\dots,b_{n}\}=ADSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}
.
Again, we either have
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{j}}
or
(b^{\prime\prime},a^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{j}}
, as required by item four of the claim.
∎

5.3.5
Non-Interference postulate

Finally, we are ready to show that, for any two syntactically disjoint AS
AS_{1}
,
AS_{2}
and their union
AS_{+}
, for the corresponding JSBAFs
\mathcal{J}_{1}
,
\mathcal{J}_{2}
and
\mathcal{J}_{+}
, any preferred labeling
L_{1}
of
\mathcal{J}_{1}
can be turned into a preferred labeling
L_{+}
of
\mathcal{J}_{+}
and any preferred labeling
L_{+}
of
\mathcal{J}_{+}
can be reduced to a preferred labeling
L_{1}
of
\mathcal{J}_{1}
.

For this, we will show three sub-results:
First, we take a preferred labeling
L
of
\mathcal{J}_{1}
and show that by
“adding”
to
in(L)
only the strict arguments of
\mathcal{J}_{2}
, we can turn
L
into an admissible labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{+}
.
Next, we will show that a preferred labeling
L_{+}
of
\mathcal{J}_{+}
can be turned into an admissible labeling
L_{1}
of
\mathcal{J}_{1}
by simply
“removing”
all arguments of
\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus\mathcal{A}_{1}
from
in(L_{+})
,
out(L_{+})
and
undec(L_{+})
.
Lastly, we will then show that, if there is any admissible labeling
L_{1}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{J}_{1}
s.t.
in(L_{1})\subseteq in(L_{1}^{\prime})
, we can
“add back”
the arguments of
in(L_{+})
that were removed in the previous step and create another admissible labeling from that.
By combining the first and second of these sub-results, we will be able to show that
\mathcal{C}_{\sigma}(AS_{1})\!\!\mid\!\!_{Atoms(AS_{1})}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{\sigma}(AS_{+})\!\!\mid\!\!_{Atoms(AS_{1})}
holds, while combining the second and third of these sub-results will prove that
\mathcal{C}_{\sigma}(AS_{1})\!\!\mid\!\!_{Atoms(AS_{1})}\supseteq\mathcal{C}_{\sigma}(AS_{+})\!\!\mid\!\!_{Atoms(AS_{1})}
holds.

The proof for turning
L_{1}
into
L_{+}
works by first creating an admissible labeling
L_{+}^{adm}
of
\mathcal{J}_{+}
s.t.
in(L_{1})\subseteq in(L_{+}^{adm})
.
To do this, we use the following definition:

Definition 18
.

Let
AS_{1}=(R_{s_{1}},R_{d_{1}},n_{1},{\leq_{r_{1}}})
,
AS_{2}=(R_{s_{2}},R_{d_{2}},n_{2},{\leq_{r_{2}}})
be two
AS
s.t.
AS_{1}||AS_{2}
and let
AS_{+}
be their union.
Furthermore, let
\mathcal{J}_{1}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{1}},{\preceq_{1}})
,
\mathcal{J}_{2}=(\mathcal{A}_{2},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{2}},{\preceq_{2}})
and
\mathcal{J}_{+}=(\mathcal{A}_{+},{\rightarrow_{+}},{\Rightarrow_{+}},{\preceq_{+}})
be the JSBAF’s corresponding to
AS_{1}
,
AS_{2}
and
AS_{+}
respectively.
Lastly, for
i,j\in\{1,2\}
with
i\neq j
, let
L_{i}\in pr(\mathcal{J}_{i})
and let
SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}}
be the strict including minimal labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{j}
.
We construct the
combined minimal labeling
of
L_{i}
and
SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}}
, denoted
L_{+}^{min}
, as follows:

\displaystyle I_{0}

\displaystyle=in(L_{i})\cup in(SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}})

\displaystyle I_{k+1}

\displaystyle=I_{k}\cup\{a\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\mid\exists(S,a)\in{\Rightarrow}_{+},S\subseteq I_{k}\}

\displaystyle in(L_{+}^{min})

\displaystyle=\bigcup\limits_{k\geq 0}I_{k}

\displaystyle O_{0}

\displaystyle=\{a\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\mid\exists(b,a)\in{\rightarrow}_{+},b\in in(L_{+}^{min})\}

\displaystyle O_{k+1}

\displaystyle=O_{k}\cup\{a\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\mid\exists(S,b)\in{\Rightarrow}_{+},a\in S,

\displaystyle\hskip 83.36636ptS\setminus\{a\}\subseteq in(L_{+}^{min}),

\displaystyle\hskip 83.36636ptb\in O_{k}\}

\displaystyle out(L_{+}^{min})

\displaystyle=\bigcup\limits_{k\geq 0}O_{k}

\displaystyle undec(L_{+}^{min})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus\big(in(L_{+}^{min})\cup out(L_{+}^{min})\big)

Note that the only arguments in
\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
that are accepted in
L_{+}^{min}
, are minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
-arguments.
Furthermore, no
“new”
arguments in
\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
\mathcal{A}_{j}
are accepted by
L_{+}^{min}
, i.e. if
a\in in(L_{+}^{min})\cap\mathcal{A}_{i}
, then
a\in in(L_{i})
and if
a\in in(L_{+}^{min})\cap\mathcal{A}_{j}
, then
a\in SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}}
.

We now prove that
L_{+}^{min}
is indeed an admissible labeling.
Later, we will use this result to show that any preferred labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{i}
can be turned into a preferred labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{+}
.
As usual, we have divided this proof into several parts to make it more accessible.

Proposition 25
.

Let
AS_{1}=(R_{s_{1}},R_{d_{1}},n_{1},{\leq_{r_{1}}})
,
AS_{2}=(R_{s_{2}},R_{d_{2}},n_{2},{\leq_{r_{2}}})
be two
AS
s.t.
AS_{1}||AS_{2}
and let
AS_{+}
be their union.
Furthermore, let
\mathcal{J}_{1}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{1}},{\preceq_{1}})
,
\mathcal{J}_{2}=(\mathcal{A}_{2},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{2}},{\preceq_{2}})
and
\mathcal{J}_{+}=(\mathcal{A}_{+},{\rightarrow_{+}},{\Rightarrow_{+}},{\preceq_{+}})
be the JSBAF’s corresponding to
AS_{1}
,
AS_{2}
and
AS_{+}
respectively.
Lastly, for
i,j\in\{1,2\}
with
i\neq j
, for any preferred labeling
L_{i}\in pr(\mathcal{J}_{i})
and for
SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}}
being the strict including minimal labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{j}
, let
L_{+}^{min}
be the combined minimal labeling of
L_{i}
and
SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}}
.
Then
L_{+}^{min}
is a labeling.

Proof.

We begin by arguing that
L_{+}^{min}
is a labeling.
Similar to the proof of Proposition
13
, the actual proof proceeds by induction over the construction of
out(L_{+}^{min})
.
However, since it is clear from the construction of
L_{+}^{min}
that we only need to ensure
in(L_{+}^{min})\cap out(L_{+}^{min})=\emptyset
and since the induction step of this proof is trivial, we focus here on the induction start.
That is, we argue why there cannot be some
a\in in(L_{+}^{min})\cap O_{0}
:
Towards a contradiction, suppose that this does not hold, i.e. there are arguments
a,b\in in(L_{+}^{min})
s.t.
(b,a)\in{\rightarrow}_{+}
.

We first note that both
a
and
b
are consistent arguments.
If they are contained in
in(L_{i})
or in
in(SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}})
, then this is easy to see because these are admissible labelings.
If they are contained in
\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
, then we can make a simple model-theoretic argument to show the consistency:
For
c\in\{a,b\}
, let
c\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
.
Towards a contradiction, assume that
c
is inconsistent, i.e. there is
\Gamma\subseteq Sub(c)^{C}
s.t.
\Gamma
is unsatisfiable.
Take
ADSub(c)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
and
ADSub(c)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}
.
Note that these sets of arguments are contained in
in(L_{i})
and
in(SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}})
respectively.
Because
L_{i}
and
SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}}
are admissible labelings, we can now infer that both
\big(ADSub(c)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\big)^{C}
and
\big(ADSub(c)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}\big)^{C}
are satisfiable, i.e. there exists interpretations
I_{1}\vDash_{M}\bigwedge\big(ADSub(c)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\big)^{C}
and
I_{2}\vDash_{M}\bigwedge\big(ADSub(c)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}\big)^{C}
.
Note that these are sets of syntactically disjoint formulas.
Now there exists an interpretation
I
s.t.
I\vDash_{M}\big(ADSub(c)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\big)^{C}
and
I\vDash_{M}\big(ADSub(c)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\big)^{C}
.
By Proposition
10
we can now infer that
\Gamma
is satisfied by
I
, contradicting our assumptions.

Now for the actual proof:
Because we used the admissible labelings
L_{i}
and
SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}}
as a starting point for the construction of
in(L_{+}^{min})
, we know that we cannot have
a,b\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
or
a,b\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
.
By Proposition
21
(which corresponds to case one of Illustration
1
), we can also infer that we cannot have
a\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
b\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
or
a\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
and
b\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
, as this would mean either
a
or
b
is attacked by a strict argument and therefore not accepted by
L_{i}
or
SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}}
respectively.
This means at least one of the arguments
a
and
b
needs to be from
\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
.

Suppose first that we have
a\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
b\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
(this corresponds to case two of Illustration
1
).
Then
a\in in(L_{i})
by construction of
L_{+}^{min}
.
From Proposition
22
, we can infer that there exists an argument
b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
s.t.
(b^{\prime},a)\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
and
CSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}=CSub(b^{\prime})
.
By construction of
L_{+}^{min}
, we have
CSub(b^{\prime})\subseteq in(L_{i})
and by admissibility of
L_{i}
, we can infer that
b^{\prime}\in in(L_{i})
must also hold.
Now
(b^{\prime},a)\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
contradicts the admissibility of
L_{i}
.
The same argument can be made for the case
a\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
and
b\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
.

Next, suppose that we have
a\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
and
b\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
(this corresponds to case three of Illustration
1
).
Note again that this means
b\in in(L_{i})
by construction of
L_{+}^{min}
.
From Proposition
23
, we can infer that there exist arguments
b^{\prime},a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
, s.t.
CSub(b^{\prime})=CSub(b)
,
CSub(a^{\prime})=CSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
and
(b^{\prime},a^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
, or that there exists arguments
b^{\prime},a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
s.t.
CSub(b^{\prime})=CSub(b)
,
CSub(a^{\prime})=ADSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
and either
(b^{\prime},a^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
or
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
.
By construction of
in(L_{+}^{min})
, we must have
CSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\subseteq in(L_{i})
.
Furthermore, since
L_{i}
was a preferred labeling, we can infer from Lemma
2
that
CSub(b)\subseteq in(L_{i})
.
Similarly, we can use Lemma
2
and
CSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\subseteq in(L_{i})
to infer that
ADSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\subseteq in(L_{i})
also holds.
Now we can use admissibility of
L_{i}
to infer
L_{i}(a^{\prime})=L_{i}(b^{\prime})=\textit{IN}
.
However, now
(b^{\prime},a^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow}_{i}
and
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
both contradict the admissibility of
L_{i}
.
The same argument holds for the case that
a\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
and
b\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
(note that
SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}}
is by definition closed under sub-arguments).

Lastly, let us suppose that we have
a,b\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
(this corresponds to case four of Illustration
1
).
The argumentation in this case is similar to the one that corresponds to case three of Illustration
1
.
However, now we need to use Proposition
24
in order to infer that there are arguments
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
or
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
for which we have
L_{i}(a^{\prime})=L_{i}(b^{\prime})=\textit{IN}
or
SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}}(a^{\prime})=SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}}(b^{\prime})=\textit{IN}
.
Either way, we can again infer a contradiction, either for
L_{i}
being admissible or for
SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}}
being admissible.
As all possible cases lead to a contradiction, we conclude that
in(L_{+}^{min})\cap O_{0}=\emptyset
must hold, just as we claimed.
∎

Proposition 26
.

Let
AS_{1}=(R_{s_{1}},R_{d_{1}},n_{1},{\leq_{r_{1}}})
,
AS_{2}=(R_{s_{2}},R_{d_{2}},n_{2},{\leq_{r_{2}}})
be two
AS
s.t.
AS_{1}||AS_{2}
and let
AS_{+}
be their union.
Furthermore, let
\mathcal{J}_{1}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{1}},{\preceq_{1}})
,
\mathcal{J}_{2}=(\mathcal{A}_{2},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{2}},{\preceq_{2}})
and
\mathcal{J}_{+}=(\mathcal{A}_{+},{\rightarrow_{+}},{\Rightarrow_{+}},{\preceq_{+}})
be the JSBAF’s corresponding to
AS_{1}
,
AS_{2}
and
AS_{+}
respectively.
Lastly, for
i,j\in\{1,2\}
with
i\neq j
, for any preferred labeling
L_{i}\in pr(\mathcal{J}_{i})
and for
SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}}
being the strict including minimal labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{j}
, let
L_{+}^{min}
be the combined minimal labeling of
L_{i}
and
SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}}
.
We have
a\in out(L_{+}^{min})
, iff
a
is legally OUT w.r.t.
L_{+}^{min}
.

Proof.

From the construction of
out(L_{+}^{min})
, it is easy to see that, if
a
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
