L_{+}^{min}
, then
a\in out(L_{+}^{min})
.
We therefore focus here on the other direction, i.e. showing that if
a\in out(L_{+}^{min})
, then
a
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{+}^{min}
.
Similar to the proof of Proposition
16
, it is not immediately clear from the construction of
out(L_{+}^{min})
, that this holds.
More precisely, consider the case that we have
x\in O_{k+1}\setminus O_{k}
.
Then there must exist some support chain
\mathcal{C}=\big\{(S_{0},b_{0}),\dots,(S_{n},b_{n})\big\}\subseteq{\Rightarrow}
s.t.
x\in S_{0}
, for all
0<k\leq n
we have
S_{k}\setminus\{b_{k-1}\}\subseteq in(L_{+}^{min})
, for
S_{0}
we have
S_{0}\setminus\{x\}\subseteq in(L_{+}^{min})
and there exists an attack
(y,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow}
with
L_{+}^{min}(y)=\textit{IN}
.
However, it is not necessarily the case that we have
x\preceq_{+}d
for all
d\in S_{0}\setminus\{x\}
.
Assume that there is
d\in S_{0}\setminus\{x\}
s.t.
d\prec_{+}x
.
As in the proof of Proposition
16
, we will show that this implies the existence of an
“alternative reason”
for why
x
is legally labeled
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{+}^{min}
.

We begin by constructing some helper-arguments:
Let
S=\big(S_{0}\setminus\{x\}\big)\cup\bigcup\limits_{0<k\leq n}\big(S_{k}\setminus\{b_{k-1}\}\big)
, i.e.
S
contains all arguments labeled
IN
along the support chain
\mathcal{C}
.
Analogous to the proof of Proposition
16
, for
\{z_{0},\dots,z_{m}\}=S
we have
ADSub(x)^{C}\vDash_{C}\lnot\bigwedge\big(ADSub(y)\cup ADSub(z_{0})\cup\dots\cup ADSub(z_{m})\big)^{C}
.
Now we construct the arguments
y^{\prime}:y,z_{0},\dots,z_{m}\rightarrow\bigwedge\{y,z_{0},\dots,z_{m}\}^{C}
and
x^{\prime}:x_{0},\dots,x_{k}\rightarrow\lnot\bigwedge\Delta
, where
\{x_{0},\dots,x_{k}\}=ADSub(x)
and
\Delta=\big(ADSub(y)\cup ADSub(z_{0})\cup\dots\cup ADSub(z_{m})\big)^{C}
.
Clearly,
x^{\prime}
gen-rebuts
y^{\prime}
.
Furthermore, because we have
d\prec_{+}x
, there must exist
r_{d}\in DR(d)\subseteq DR(y^{\prime})
s.t. for all
r_{x}\in DR(x)=DR(x^{\prime})
, we have
r_{d}\leq_{r}^{+}r_{x}
.
This implies
y^{\prime}\preceq_{+}x^{\prime}
, therefore
x^{\prime}\not\prec_{+}y^{\prime}
and thus
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow}_{+}
.
Note that by construction of
L_{+}^{min}
,
y^{\prime}\in in(L_{+}^{min})
holds.

Next, we make a case distinction based on the origins of
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}
:
Suppose first that we have
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
.
By construction of
in(L_{+}^{min})
, we can infer that
y^{\prime}\in in(L_{i})
must hold and by admissibility of
L_{i}
, this implies
x^{\prime}\in out(L_{i})
.
By construction of
x^{\prime}
, we obviously have
DR(x^{\prime})\subseteq DR(x)
and
ADSub(x^{\prime})^{C}\subseteq ADSub(x)^{C}
.
By assumption,
L_{i}
is a preferred labeling, which means it is admissible.
Now we can apply Proposition
15
to infer
x\in out(L_{i})
, which implies that
x
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{i}
either due to an attack or due to a support chain.
Because
in(L_{i})\subseteq in(L_{+}^{min})
we can now infer that
x
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{+}^{min}
as required.
The case
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
can be proven analogously.

Next, assume that we have
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
and
y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
(this corresponds to case one of Illustration
1
).
By Proposition
21
, we can now infer that either
x^{\prime}
or
y^{\prime}
are inconsistent.
Similar to before, we can infer from the construction of
L_{+}^{min}
that
y^{\prime}\in in(L_{i})
must hold.
Since
L_{i}
is an admissible labeling,
y^{\prime}
must be consistent, therefore
x^{\prime}
must be inconsistent.
This means there exists a strict argument
\overline{x^{\prime}}
and an attack
(\overline{x^{\prime}},x)\in{\rightarrow_{j}}
.
By Proposition
11
, we can use this strict argument to construct a strict argument which is attacking
x
.
This means
x
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{+}^{min}
as required.
The case
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
can be proven analogously.

Next, assume that we have
y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
, while
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
holds (this corresponds to case three of Illustration
1
).
Note that by construction of
in(L_{+}^{min})
, this implies that
y^{\prime}
is a minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
-argument.
Furthermore, we want to point out that we can assume both
y^{\prime}
and
x^{\prime}
are consistent:
For
y^{\prime}
we can make a model-theoretic argument similar as in the proof of Proposition
25
, while if
x^{\prime}
is inconsistent then we can immediately infer that
x
must be inconsistent as well.
By Proposition
23
, we can now have two cases:

1.

There exists arguments
x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
s.t.
CSub(x^{\prime\prime})=CSub(x^{\prime})
,
CSub(y^{\prime\prime})=CSub(y^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
and
(x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
.

2.

There exist arguments
x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
s.t.
CSub(x^{\prime\prime})=CSub(x^{\prime})
,
CSub(y^{\prime\prime})=ADSub(y^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
and either
(x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
, or
(y^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}

In the first case, we can infer from
y^{\prime}\in in(L_{+}^{min})
that
CSub(y^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\subseteq in(L_{i})
must hold.
Because
L_{i}
is an admissible labeling, we can now infer
y^{\prime\prime}\in in(L_{i})
, which implies
x^{\prime\prime}\in out(L_{i})
.
We want to point out that by construction of
x^{\prime}
and
x^{\prime\prime}
, we have
ADSub(x)=ADSub(x^{\prime})=ADSub(x^{\prime\prime})
and
DR(x)=DR(x^{\prime})=DR(x^{\prime\prime})
.
In particular, this means
DR(x^{\prime\prime})\subseteq DR(x)
and
ADSub(x^{\prime\prime})^{C}\subseteq ADSub(x)^{C}
.
Since
L_{i}
is an admissible labeling, we can now apply Proposition
15
again to infer that
L_{i}(x)=\textit{OUT}
must hold.
This implies that there is an attack or a support chain s.t.
x
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{i}
.
By
in(L_{i})\subseteq in(L_{+}^{max})
we can now infer that
x
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{+}^{min}
as required.

In the second case, we first use Lemma
2
(and the fact that
SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}}
is by definition closed under sub-arguments) to infer that
ADSub(y^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\subseteq in(L_{i})
holds.
This, in turn, implies
y^{\prime\prime}\in in(L_{i})
.
Now, if
(y^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
, we can use the argument
y^{\prime\prime}
as a reason for why
x^{\prime\prime}
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{i}
.
On the other hand, if
(x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
, we can use the admissibility of
L_{i}
to infer that
x^{\prime\prime}
must be legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{i}
.
Either way, we can again use the reason for why
x^{\prime\prime}
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{i}
to construct a reason for why
x
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{i}
.
By
in(L_{i})\subseteq in(L_{+}^{min})
we can infer that
x
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{+}^{min}
as required.
The case
y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
and
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
can be proven analogously.

For our last few cases, suppose that we have
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
.
Note that
x^{\prime}
doesn’t necessarily need to be a minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
-argument.
Thus, we first construct such a minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
-argument so that we can apply either Proposition
22
or Proposition
24
:
Remember that
x^{\prime}
is of the form
x^{\prime}:x_{0},\dots,x_{k}\rightarrow\lnot\bigwedge\Delta
, where for each
0\leq l\leq k
, we have
x_{l}\in ADSub(x)
.
This implies
Atoms(x_{l}^{C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{i})
or
Atoms(x_{l}^{C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{j})
for each of these arguments.
Note that, if any of the arguments
x_{l}
is inconsistent, then
x
is also inconsistent.
This implies
x
is attacked by a strict argument and thus legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{+}^{min}
because of this strict attacker.
We therefore assume that all the arguments
x_{l}
are consistent.
Now we can use Proposition
19
to infer that for each
0\leq l\leq k
, there exists an argument
x_{l}^{\prime}
which is the reduced version of
x_{l}
either w.r.t.
AS_{i}
or w.r.t.
AS_{j}
(depending on whether
Atoms(x_{l}^{C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{i})
or
Atoms(x_{l}^{C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{j})
).
With these reduced versions, we can construct the argument
x^{\prime\prime}
which is of the form
x^{\prime\prime}:x_{0}^{\prime},\dots x_{k}^{\prime}\rightarrow\lnot\bigwedge\Delta
.
Note that we have
DR(x^{\prime\prime})\subseteq DR(x^{\prime})=DR(x)
, which implies
(x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
.
Furthermore,
x^{\prime\prime}
is a minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
-argument and by construction of
x^{\prime}
and
x^{\prime\prime}
, we have
ADSub(x^{\prime\prime})^{C}\subseteq ADSub(x)^{C}
.
Lastly, we can assume that
x^{\prime\prime}
is consistent, since otherwise
x
would be inconsistent and legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{+}^{min}
as required.

Now, assume that we have
y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
(this corresponds to case two of Illustration
1
).
By construction of
y^{\prime}
, this implies
y^{\prime}\in in(L_{i})\subseteq in(L_{+}^{min})
.
Furthermore, we can infer that
y^{\prime}
is consistent.
Because
x^{\prime\prime}
is a (consistent) minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
-argument, we can now use Proposition
22
to infer that there exists an argument
x^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
s.t.
CSub(x^{\prime\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}=CSub(x^{\prime\prime\prime})
and
(x^{\prime\prime\prime},y^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
.
By admissibility of
L_{i}
, we can now infer that
x^{\prime\prime\prime}\in out(L_{i})
must hold.
This implies
x^{\prime\prime\prime}
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{i}
, which means there is either an attack
(u,x^{\prime\prime\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
or a support chain
\mathcal{C}=\big\{(S_{0},b_{0}),\dots,(S_{n},b_{n})\big\}
giving us a reason for why
x^{\prime\prime\prime}
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{i}
.
Note that, by
CSub(x^{\prime\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}=CSub(x^{\prime\prime\prime})
, we have
DR(x^{\prime\prime\prime})\subseteq DR(x^{\prime\prime})\subseteq DR(x)
and
ADSub(x^{\prime\prime\prime})^{C}\subseteq ADSub(x^{\prime\prime})^{C}\subseteq ADSub(x)^{C}
.

Suppose first that we have an attack
(u,x^{\prime\prime\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
with
u\in in(L_{i})
.
Then
u
is either undercutting or gen-rebutting
x^{\prime\prime\prime}
.
If
u
is undercutting
x^{\prime\prime\prime}
, then we can use
DR(x^{\prime\prime\prime})\subseteq DR(x)
to infer that
(u,x)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
also holds.
If
u
is gen-rebutting
x^{\prime\prime\prime}
, then we can again use Proposition
11
as well as
ADSub(x^{\prime\prime\prime})^{C}\subseteq ADSub(x)^{C}
and
DR(x^{\prime\prime\prime})\subseteq DR(x)
to infer that there exists
u^{\prime}\in in(L_{i})
s.t.
(u^{\prime},x)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
.
Either way, there exists an attacker of
x
which is labeled
IN
in
L_{i}
.
By
in(L_{i})\subseteq in(L_{+}^{min})
we can infer that
x
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{+}^{min}
as required.

Now suppose that
x^{\prime\prime\prime}
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{i}
due to a support chain
\mathcal{C}=\big\{(S_{0},b_{0}),\dots,(S_{n},b_{n})\big\}
and an attack
(c,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
with
x^{\prime\prime\prime}\in S_{0}
and
L_{i}(c)=\textit{IN}
.
Let
S=S_{0}\setminus\{x^{\prime\prime\prime}\}\cup\bigcup\limits_{0<k\leq n}S_{k}\setminus\{b_{k-1}\}
be the set of all arguments labeled
IN
in this support chain.
If
(c,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
is the result of an undercut, we can use
S\subseteq in(L_{i})
to infer that
c
must be undercutting
x^{\prime\prime\prime}
.
By
DR(x^{\prime\prime\prime})\subseteq DR(x)
, we can infer that
c
is also undercutting
x
and since
in(L_{i})\subseteq in(L_{+}^{max})
, this means
x
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{+}^{min}
as required.
Now suppose that
(c,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
is the result of a gen-rebut.
We construct the argument
b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
which is of the form
b^{\prime}:x,d_{0},\dots d_{m}\rightarrow\bigwedge\{x,d_{0},\dots,d_{m}\}^{C}
, for
\{d_{0},\dots,d_{m}\}=S
.
Furthermore, we use Proposition
11
to construct the argument
c^{\prime}
which is of the form
c^{\prime}:c\rightarrow\lnot\bigwedge ADSub(b_{n})^{C}
.
Note that we have
ADSub(b_{n})^{C}\subseteq ADSub(b^{\prime})^{C}
because
ADSub(x^{\prime\prime\prime})^{C}\subseteq ADSub(x)^{C}
.
This means
c^{\prime}
is also gen-rebutting
b^{\prime}
.
Because
(c,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
is the result of a gen-rebut, we have
c\not\prec_{i}b_{n}
.
Since
DR(x^{\prime\prime\prime})\subseteq DR(x)
, we can now infer that
c\not\prec_{+}b^{\prime}
also holds.
Because
DR(c)=DR(c^{\prime})
this means
(c^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
.
By admissibility of
L_{i}
, we can infer
c^{\prime}\in in(L_{i})\subseteq in(L_{+}^{min})
.
By construction of
L_{+}^{min}
we now have
b^{\prime}\in O_{0}\subseteq out(L_{+}^{min})
.
We can make a simple inductive argument to show that we have
x^{\prime\prime\prime}\preceq_{i}d
for all
d\in S
.
This means for all
d\in S
, there is
r_{x}\in DR(x^{\prime\prime\prime})
s.t. for all
r_{d}\in DR(d)
,
r_{x}\leq_{i}r_{d}
.
Because
DR(x^{\prime\prime\prime})\subseteq DR(x)
and because
{\leq_{r_{i}}}\subseteq{\leq_{+}}
by construction of
AS_{+}
from
AS_{i}
and
AS_{j}
, we now have
x\preceq_{+}d
for all
d\in S
.
Since
S\subseteq in(L_{i})\subseteq in(L_{+}^{min})
, we can now infer that
x
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{+}^{min}
due to the support chain
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\big\{(S\cup\{x\},b^{\prime})\big\}
and the attack
(c^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
.
The case
y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
can be proven analogously.

Lastly, suppose that we have
y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
(this corresponds to case four of Illustration
1
).
Then both
x^{\prime}
and
y^{\prime}
are minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
-arguments.
Note that we can argue for the consistency of
y^{\prime}
and
x^{\prime}
as before:
For
y^{\prime}
we can make a model-theoretic argument for its consistency, while inconsistency of
x^{\prime}
implies that
x
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{+}^{min}
.
Now we can use Proposition
24
to infer that one of the following four cases must hold:

1.

There are
x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
s.t.
CSub(x^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}=CSub(x^{\prime\prime})
,
CSub(y^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}=CSub(y^{\prime\prime})
and
(x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
.

2.

There are
x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
s.t.
CSub(x^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}=CSub(x^{\prime\prime})
,
CSub(y^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}=CSub(y^{\prime\prime})
and
(x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{j}}
.

3.

There are
x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
s.t.
CSub(x^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}=CSub(x^{\prime\prime})
,
ADSub(y^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}=CSub(y^{\prime\prime})
and either
(x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
or
(y^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
.

4.

There are
x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
s.t.
CSub(x^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}=CSub(x^{\prime\prime})
,
ADSub(y^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}=CSub(y^{\prime\prime})
and either
(x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{j}}
or
(y^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{j}}
.

With the same reasoning that we used to prove the cases which corresponded to cases two and three of Illustration
1
, we can show that in all of the four cases described above,
x^{\prime\prime}
needs to be legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{i}
or
L_{j}
(depending on the specific case).
Because we have
DR(x^{\prime\prime})\subseteq DR(x)
,
ADSub(x^{\prime\prime})^{C}\subseteq ADSub(x)^{C}
and
\big(in(L_{i})\cup in(SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}})\big)\subseteq in(L_{+}^{min})
, we can use this to infer that
x
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{+}^{min}
, similar to the cases corresponding to cases two and three of Illustration
1
.

We conclude that regardless of the origins of
x^{\prime}
and
y^{\prime}
, if
x\in out(L_{+}^{min})
, then
x
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{+}^{min}
.
∎

Proposition 27
.

Let
AS_{1}=(R_{s_{1}},R_{d_{1}},n_{1},{\leq_{r_{1}}})
,
AS_{2}=(R_{s_{2}},R_{d_{2}},n_{2},{\leq_{r_{2}}})
be two
AS
s.t.
AS_{1}||AS_{2}
and let
AS_{+}
be their union.
Furthermore, let
\mathcal{J}_{1}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{1}},{\preceq_{1}})
,
\mathcal{J}_{2}=(\mathcal{A}_{2},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{2}},{\preceq_{2}})
and
\mathcal{J}_{+}=(\mathcal{A}_{+},{\rightarrow_{+}},{\Rightarrow_{+}},{\preceq_{+}})
be the JSBAF’s corresponding to
AS_{1}
,
AS_{2}
and
AS_{+}
respectively.
Lastly, for
i,j\in\{1,2\}
with
i\neq j
, for any preferred labeling
L_{i}\in pr(\mathcal{J}_{i})
and for
SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}}
being the strict including minimal labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{j}
, let
L_{+}^{min}
be the combined minimal labeling of
L_{i}
and
SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}}
.
If
a\in in(L_{+}^{min})
, then
a
is legally IN w.r.t.
L_{+}^{min}
.

Proof.

Let
a\in in(L_{+}^{min})
be an accepted argument.
We first consider the supports
(S,b)\in{\Rightarrow_{+}}
where
a\in S
holds:
By construction of
in(L_{+}^{min})
, if
S\subseteq in(L_{+}^{min})
, then
b\in in(L_{+}^{min})
also holds.
Furthermore, by construction of
out(L_{+}^{min})
, if
|S\setminus in(L_{+}^{min})|=1
while
b\in out(L_{+}^{min})
, then
S\setminus in(L_{+}^{min})\in out(L_{+}^{min})
also holds.
Therefore, the conditions of items
1.a
to
1.c
of Definition
2
are always satisfied and we only need to show that all attackers of
a
are labeled
OUT
in
L_{+}^{min}
.
Note that, when we argued that
L_{+}^{min}
is a labeling, we have already shown that, if
a\in in(L_{+}^{min})
and there is
(b,a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
, then
L_{+}^{min}(b)\neq\textit{IN}
.
Thus, we only have left to prove that
L_{+}^{min}(b)\neq\textit{UNDEC}
.
Towards a contradiction, suppose that this does not hold.
We proceed in a similar matter as for the proof that showed
L_{+}^{min}
is a labeling by considering all possible cases for the origins of
a
and
b
.

First, suppose that
a,b\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
(or
a,b\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
).
Then
b
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{i}
(or
SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}}
respectively) and because
in(L_{i})\cup in(SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}})\subseteq in(L_{+}^{min})
, we can infer that
b
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{+}^{min}
.
By Proposition
26
this implies
L_{+}^{min}(b)=\textit{OUT}
, contradicting our assumption
L_{+}^{min}(b)=\textit{UNDEC}
.
Next, suppose that
a\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
b\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
(or
a\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
and
b\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
), which corresponds to case one of Illustration
1
.
Then by Proposition
21
, either
a
or
b
are legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{+}^{min}
, which again means either
L_{+}^{min}(a)=\textit{OUT}
or
L_{+}^{min}(b)=\textit{OUT}
, contradicting our assumptions.

Now suppose that
a\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
, while
b\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
, which corresponds to case two of Illustration
1
(we leave out the case
a\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
, while
b\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
, as it can be proven analogous).
From
(b,a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
, we can infer that
b
undercuts or gen-rebuts
a
.
In the first case, we have
Atoms(b^{C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{i})
.
In the second case, we can use Proposition
11
to infer that there exists
b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{+}
which is of the form
b^{\prime}:b\rightarrow\lnot\bigwedge ADSub(a)^{C}
.
Note that we have
Atoms(b^{\prime C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{i})
.
Now, regardless of whether the attack
(b,a)
was the result of an undercut or a gen-rebut, there is some
(x,a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
s.t.
Atoms(x)\subseteq Atoms(AS_{i})
.
By Proposition
19
, we can infer that there exists a reduced version of
x
w.r.t.
AS_{i}
, i.e. some
x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
for which we have
x^{C}=x^{\prime C}
and
DR(x^{\prime})\subseteq DR(x)
.
Now
x^{\prime}
undercuts or gen-rebuts
a
.
If
x^{\prime}
gen-rebuts
a
, then we note that
(x,a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
means
x\not\prec a
, which in turn implies
x^{\prime}\not\prec a
.
Thus we can infer that
(x^{\prime},a)\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
must hold, regardless of whether
x^{\prime}
undercuts or gen-rebuts
a
.
Since
L_{i}
was an admissible labeling and
a\in in(L_{i})
, we can infer that
L_{i}(x^{\prime})=\textit{OUT}
holds and because
in(L_{i})\subseteq in(L_{+}^{min})
, we must also have
L_{+}^{min}(x^{\prime})=\textit{OUT}
by construction of
L_{+}^{min}
.
Now we can use Lemma
3
and Proposition
26
, to infer that
L_{+}^{min}(x)=\textit{OUT}
also holds, contradicting our assumption that
a
is attacked by an argument which is labeled
UNDEC
.
This proves case two of Illustration
1
.

Now, suppose that we have
a\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
and
b\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
, which corresponds to case three of Illustration
1
(we again leave out the case
a\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
and
b\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
, as it can be proven analogous).
Suppose that
(b,a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
is the result of an undercut.
Then this attack must also be directed towards some
x\in CSub(a)
and by construction of
in(L_{+}^{min})
, we have
x\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
or
x\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
.
In the first case, we can again infer that
x
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{i}
by admissibility of
L_{i}
and by construction of
L_{+}^{min}
, while in the second case either
x
or
b
must be legally
OUT
w.r.t.
SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}}
or
L_{i}
respectively.
All of these options contradict our assumptions for the labels of
a
and
b
.

Now suppose that
(b,a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
is the result of a gen-rebut.
By Proposition
11
, we can again infer that there must be an argument
b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
of the form
b^{\prime}:b\rightarrow\lnot\bigwedge ADSub(a)^{C}
.
Since
DR(b)=DR(b^{\prime})
, it is clear that
(b^{\prime},a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
.
We can make a simple model-theoretic argument to show that
\big(ADSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}\big)^{C}\cup\{b^{\prime C}\}\vDash_{C}\lnot\bigwedge\big(ADSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\big)^{C}
.
With this, we can construct the argument
c\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
and the arguments
a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
, as follows:
c:a_{j_{0}},\dots,a_{j_{m}},b^{\prime}\rightarrow\lnot\bigwedge\big(ADSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\big)^{C}
,
a^{\prime}:a_{i_{0}},\dots,a_{i_{n}}\rightarrow\bigwedge\big(ADSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\big)^{C}
and
a^{\prime\prime}:a^{\prime}\rightarrow\lnot\lnot\bigwedge\big(ADSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\big)^{C}
, where
\{a_{j_{0}},\dots,a_{j_{m}}\}=ADSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}
and
\{a_{i_{0}},\dots,a_{i_{n}}\}=ADSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
.
Note that we have
ADSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\subseteq in(L_{i})
by Lemma
2
and
ADSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}\subseteq in(SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}})
by definition of the strict including minimal labeling.
Clearly,
c
gen-rebuts
a^{\prime}
and
a^{\prime\prime}
gen-rebuts
c
.
It is also clear that either
(c,a^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
or
(a^{\prime\prime},c)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
must hold.

Suppose first that we have
(c,a^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
.
From
ADSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\subseteq in(L_{i})
, we can infer
a^{\prime}\in in(L_{i})\subseteq in(L_{+}^{min})
.
Now the attack
(c,a^{\prime})
corresponds to case two of Illustration
1
.
We have argued above, that in this case,
a^{\prime}\in in(L_{+}^{min})
implies
c\in out(L_{+}^{min})
.
Since
ADSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}=\{a_{j_{0}},\dots,a_{j_{m}}\}\subseteq in(L_{+}^{min})
, we can infer from the construction of
out(L_{+}^{min})
that we must have
b^{\prime}\in out(L_{+}^{min})
, which also implies
b\in out(L_{+}^{min})
.
However, now
b\in out(L_{+}^{min})
contradicts our assumption
L_{+}^{min}(b)=\textit{UNDEC}
.
Now suppose that we have
(a^{\prime\prime},c)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
.
From
ADSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}=\{a_{i_{0}},\dots,a_{i_{n}}\}\subseteq in(L_{i})
, we can infer that
a^{\prime\prime}\in in(L_{i})\subseteq in(L_{+}^{min})
must hold.
Now
c
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{+}^{min}
and by Proposition
26
, this implies
L_{+}^{min}(c)=\textit{OUT}
, which in turn implies that
b^{\prime}
and
b
are legally
OUT
.
Again, we can now infer
L_{+}^{min}(b)=\textit{OUT}
, which contradicts our assumption
L_{+}^{min}(b)=\textit{UNDEC}
.
This proves case three of Illustration
1
.

Finally, suppose that we have
a,b\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
, which corresponds to case four of Illustration
1
.
First, let us assume that
(b,a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
is the result of an undercut.
Then this attack must also be directed towards some
a^{\prime}\in CSub(a)\in\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j}
.
This corresponds to case two of Illustration
1
.
By the construction of
in(L_{+}^{min})
, we can infer
a^{\prime}\in in(L_{+}^{min})
from
a\in in(L_{+}^{min})
.
We have argued above that this implies
L_{+}^{min}(b)=\textit{OUT}
, contradicting the assumption
L_{+}^{min}(b)=\textit{UNDEC}
.

Now suppose that
(b,a)\in¸{\rightarrow_{+}}
is the result of a gen-rebut.
The proof for this case proceeds analogous for that of case three of Illustration
1
, which we have proven above:
We construct the arguments
b^{\prime}:b\rightarrow\lnot\bigwedge ADSub(a)^{C}
(by Proposition
11
), and with the sets
ADSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}=\{a_{j_{0}},\dots,a_{j_{n}}\}
and
ADSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}=\{a_{i_{0}},\dots,a_{i_{m}}\}
we construct the argugment
c:a_{j_{0}},\dots,a_{j_{m}},b^{\prime}\rightarrow\lnot\big(ADSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\big)^{C}
, the argument
a^{\prime}:a_{i_{0}},\dots,a_{i_{n}}\rightarrow\bigwedge\big(ADSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\big)^{C}
and the argument
a^{\prime\prime}:a^{\prime}\rightarrow\lnot\lnot\bigwedge\big(ADSub(a)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}\big)^{C}
.
Note that
c\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
, while
a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
.
Furthermore, we have
a^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime}\in in(L_{i})\subseteq in(L_{+}^{min})
by Lemma
2
and by admissibility of
L_{i}
.
We again either have
(c,a^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
or
(a^{\prime\prime},c)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
.
If
(c,a^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
, then this corresponds to case two of Illustration
1
, for which we have argued that
L_{+}^{min}(c)=\textit{OUT}
, which – together with
a_{j_{0}},\dots,a_{j_{n}}\subseteq in(SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}})\subseteq in(L_{+}^{min})
– implies that
b^{\prime}
and
b
are legally
OUT
, which means
L_{+}^{min}(b)=\textit{OUT}
.
On the other hand, if
(a^{\prime\prime},c)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
, then we can infer that
c
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{+}^{min}
, which again implies
b^{\prime}
and
b
are legally
OUT
, thus
L_{+}^{min}(b)=\textit{OUT}
as required.

We conclude:
If
a\in in(L_{+}^{min})
, then
a
is legally
IN
w.r.t.
L_{+}^{min}
.
∎

Proposition 28
.

Let
AS_{1}=(R_{s_{1}},R_{d_{1}},n_{1},{\leq_{r_{1}}})
,
AS_{2}=(R_{s_{2}},R_{d_{2}},n_{2},{\leq_{r_{2}}})
be two
AS
s.t.
AS_{1}||AS_{2}
and let
AS_{+}
be their union.
Furthermore, let
\mathcal{J}_{1}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{1}},{\preceq_{1}})
,
\mathcal{J}_{2}=(\mathcal{A}_{2},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{2}},{\preceq_{2}})
and
\mathcal{J}_{+}=(\mathcal{A}_{+},{\rightarrow_{+}},{\Rightarrow_{+}},{\preceq_{+}})
be the JSBAF’s corresponding to
AS_{1}
,
AS_{2}
and
AS_{+}
respectively.
Lastly, for
i,j\in\{1,2\}
with
i\neq j
, for any preferred labeling
L_{i}\in pr(\mathcal{J}_{i})
and for
SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}}
being the strict including minimal labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{j}
, let
L_{+}^{min}
be the combined minimal labeling of
L_{i}
and
SIM_{\mathcal{J}_{j}}
.
Then
L_{+}^{min}
is an admissible labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{+}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
25
we know that
L_{+}^{min}
is a labeling.
By Proposition
26
we know that
a\in out(L_{+}^{min})
iff
a
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{+}^{min}
.
By Proposition
27
we know that
a\in in(L_{+}^{min})
implies
a
is legally
IN
w.r.t.
L_{+}^{min}
.
Lastly, it is clear from the definition of
in(L_{+}^{min})
that
STR_{\mathcal{J}_{+}}\subseteq in(L_{+}^{min})
holds.
∎

With this, we have shown that any preferred labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{i}
can be turned into an admissible labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{+}
.
For the next step, we will show that any preferred labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{+}
can be turned into an admissible labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{i}
.
We begin by defining the
restriction
of a labeling:

Definition 19
.

Let
AS_{1}=(R_{s_{1}},R_{d_{1}},n_{1},{\leq_{r_{1}}})
,
AS_{2}=(R_{s_{2}},R_{d_{2}},n_{2},{\leq_{r_{2}}})
be two
AS
s.t.
AS_{1}||AS_{2}
and let
AS_{+}
be their union.
Let
\mathcal{J}_{1}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{1}},{\preceq_{1}})
,
\mathcal{J}_{2}=(\mathcal{A}_{2},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{2}},{\preceq_{2}})
and
\mathcal{J}_{+}=(\mathcal{A}_{+},{\rightarrow_{+}},{\Rightarrow_{+}},{\preceq_{+}})
be the JSBAFs corresponding to
AS_{1}
,
AS_{2}
and
AS_{+}
respectively.
Furthermore, let
L_{+}
be a labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{+}
.
For any set of arguments
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{+}
, we define the
restriction
of
L_{+}
to
\mathcal{A}
, denoted
L_{+}\!\!\mid\!\!_{\mathcal{A}}
, as the following labeling:

in(L_{+}\!\!\mid\!\!_{\mathcal{A}})=in(L_{+})\cap\mathcal{A}
,
out(L_{+}\!\!\mid\!\!_{\mathcal{A}})=out(L_{+})\cap\mathcal{A}
and
undec(L_{+}\!\!\mid\!\!_{\mathcal{A}})=undec(L_{+})\cap\mathcal{A}
.

Proposition 29
.

Let
AS_{1}=(R_{s_{1}},R_{d_{1}},n_{1},{\leq_{r_{1}}})
,
AS_{2}=(R_{s_{2}},R_{d_{2}},n_{2},{\leq_{r_{2}}})
be two
AS
s.t.
AS_{1}||AS_{2}
and let
AS_{+}
be their union.
Let
\mathcal{J}_{1}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{1}},{\preceq_{1}})
,
\mathcal{J}_{2}=(\mathcal{A}_{2},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{2}},{\preceq_{2}})
and
\mathcal{J}_{+}=(\mathcal{A}_{+},{\rightarrow_{+}},{\Rightarrow_{+}},{\preceq_{+}})
be the JSBAFs corresponding to
AS_{1}
,
AS_{2}
and
AS_{+}
respectively.
Furthermore, let
L_{+}\in pr(\mathcal{J}_{+})
be a preferred labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{+}
and for any
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
L_{i}=L_{+}\!\!\mid\!\!_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}
be the restriction of
L_{+}
to
\mathcal{A}_{i}
.
Then
L_{i}\in adm(\mathcal{J}_{i})
.

Proof.

It is clear that
L_{i}
is a labeling (i.e. that every argument receives exactly one label), that all strict arguments are accepted in
L_{i}
, that
a\in in(L_{i})
implies
a
is legally
IN
w.r.t.
L_{i}
and that, if
a
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{i}
, then
L_{i}(a)=\textit{OUT}
.
Therefore, we only need to show that
a\in out(L_{i})
implies
a
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{i}
.
The case is clear if
L_{+}(a)=\textit{OUT}
due to an attacker
b\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
or a support chain
\mathcal{C}=\{(S_{0},b_{0}),...(S_{n},b_{n})\}\subseteq{\Rightarrow_{i}}
with
a\in S_{0}
.
We therefore assume that these cases do not hold.

First, suppose that
L_{+}(a)=\textit{OUT}
because of an attack
(b,a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
, where
b\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
L_{+}(b)=\textit{IN}
.
We make a case distinction for the origins of
b
:
If
b\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
, then we know by Proposition
21
that either
a
or
b
are legally
OUT
due to a strict argument.
By assumption,
L_{+}(b)=\textit{IN}
and
L_{+}
was a preferred labeling, therefore we must have that
a
is legally
OUT
due to a strict argument
\overline{a}
.
This argument
\overline{a}
is also present in
\mathcal{A}_{i}
, therefore
a
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{i}
as required.

Next, suppose that
L_{+}(a)=\textit{OUT}
due to some attacker
b\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
with
L_{+}(b)=\textit{IN}
.
We make a distinction regarding for the reason of
(b,a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
:
If this attack results from an undercut, then we have
Atoms(b^{C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{i})
.
Let
b^{\prime}
be a reduced version of
b
w.r.t.
AS_{i}
.
Then
b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
b^{C}=b^{\prime C}
, i.e.
b^{\prime}
undercuts
a
.
By Lemma
3
we can infer that
L_{+}(b^{\prime})=L_{i}(b^{\prime})=\textit{IN}
, which means
a
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{i}
as required.

Now assume
(b,a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
is the result of a gen-rebut.By Proposition
11
we infer that there exists an argument
b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{+}
of the form
b^{\prime}:b\rightarrow\lnot\bigwedge ADSub(a)^{C}
and an attack
(b^{\prime},a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
.
Now we take the sets
\{b_{i_{0}},\dots,b_{i_{m}}\}=CSub(b^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}=CSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
and
\{b_{j_{0}},\dots,b_{j_{n}}\}=CSub(b^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}=CSub(b)\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}
.
Note that
Atoms(b_{k}^{C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{i})
for
0\leq k\leq m
and
Atoms(b_{l}^{C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{j})
for
0\leq l\leq n
, while
Atoms(b^{\prime C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{i})
.
Furthermore, note that
\{b_{i_{0}},\dots,b_{i_{m}}\}^{C}\cup\{b_{j_{0}},\dots,b_{j_{n}}\}^{C}\vDash_{C}b^{\prime C}
.
By Proposition
18
, we can now infer that
\{b_{0},...,b_{m}\}^{C}\vDash_{C}\lnot\bigwedge ADSub(a)^{C}
.

Next, let
b_{i_{0}}^{\prime},\dots,b_{i_{m}}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
be reduced versions of
b_{i_{0}},\dots,b_{i_{m}}
w.r.t.
AS_{i}
.
We construct the argument
\widetilde{b}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
as follows:

\widetilde{b}:b_{i_{0}}^{\prime},\dots,b_{i_{m}}^{\prime}\rightarrow\lnot\bigwedge ADSub(a)^{C}
.
It is clear that
\widetilde{b}
gen-rebuts
a
.
Since each
b_{i_{k}}^{\prime}
is a reduced version of some
b_{i_{k}}\in CSub(b)
, we have
DR(b_{i_{k}}^{\prime})\subseteq DR(b)
, therefore
DR(\widetilde{b})\subseteq DR(b)
.
By assumption, we have
(b,a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
as the result of a gen-rebut, therefore
b\not\prec a
.
Now
\widetilde{b}\not\prec a
also holds and therefore
(\widetilde{b},a)\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
.
Since
b\in in(L_{+})
by assumption, we can use Lemma
2
to infer
\{b_{i_{0}},\dots b_{i_{m}}\}\subseteq in(L_{+})
.
Now we can use Lemma
3
to infer that
\{b_{i_{0}}^{\prime},\dots,b_{i_{m}}^{\prime}\}\subseteq in(L_{+})
holds.
By admissibility of
L_{+}
we now have
\widetilde{b}\in in(L_{+})
and by construction of
L_{i}
from
L_{+}
we have
\widetilde{b}\in in(L_{i})
.
Now
(\widetilde{b},a)\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
and
L_{i}(\widetilde{b})=\textit{IN}
, thus
a
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{i}
as required.

Lastly, assume that
L_{+}(a)=\textit{OUT}
because of a support chain
\mathcal{C}=\big\{(S_{0},b_{0}),\dots,(S_{n},b_{n})\big\}\subseteq{\Rightarrow_{+}}
, with
a\in S_{0}
,
(c,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
,
L_{+}(c)=\textit{IN}
,
L_{+}(b_{k})=\textit{OUT}
for
0\leq k\leq n
,
S_{0}\setminus\{a\}\subseteq in(L_{+})
,
S_{k}\setminus\{b_{k-1}\}\subseteq in(L_{+})
for
0<k\leq n
and
a\preceq d
for all
d\in S_{0}\setminus\{a\}
.
We will show that this implies that there is an attack
(\widetilde{c},a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
with
L_{+}(\widetilde{c})=\textit{IN}
.
As we have argued above, this implies
a
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{i}
.
We show the existance of such an argument
\widetilde{c}
via induction over
n\in\mathbb{N}
for
n
being the length of the support chain
\mathcal{C}
.

Induction start:
n=1
, i.e.
\mathcal{C}=\big\{(S_{0},b_{0})\big\}
.
Let
S_{0}=\{a,a_{0},\dots,a_{m}\}
.
Suppose first that the attack
(c,b_{0})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
is the result of an undercut.
Then this attack must also be directed towards one of the arguments in
S_{0}
.
By
S_{0}\setminus\{a\}\subseteq in(L_{+})
, we can infer that
c
must be undercutting
a
.
Now we have
(c,a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
and
L_{+}(c)=\textit{IN}
as required.

Next, suppose that
(c,b_{0})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
is the result of a gen-rebut.
We first use Proposition
11
to infer that there exists
c^{\prime}
of the form
c^{\prime}:c\rightarrow\lnot\bigwedge ADSub(b_{n})^{C}
.
It is easy to see that we have
\{c^{\prime}\}^{C}\cup\big(\bigcup\limits_{0\leq k\leq m}ADSub(a_{k})\big)^{C}\vDash_{C}\lnot\bigwedge ADSub(a)^{C}
.
With this, we construct the argument
c^{\prime\prime}
as
c^{\prime\prime}:c_{0},\dots,c_{l}\rightarrow\lnot\bigwedge ADSub(a)^{C}
, where
\{c_{0},\dots,c_{l}\}=\{c^{\prime}\}\cup\bigcup\limits_{0\leq k\leq m}ADSub(a_{k})
.
Clearly,
c^{\prime\prime}
gen-rebuts
a
.
Furthermore, we have
c^{\prime}\in in(L_{+})
by admissibility of
L_{+}
and – since the arguments
a_{0},\dots,a_{m}
are labeled
IN
by assumption –
\big(\bigcup\limits_{0\leq k\leq m}ADSub(a_{k})\big)\subseteq in(L_{+})
by Lemma
2
.
By admissibility of
L_{+}
we can now infer that
L_{+}(c^{\prime\prime})=\textit{IN}
also holds.

Now, take
r_{a}\in DR(a)
s.t.
r_{a}
is minimal (w.r.t.
\leq_{r}^{+}
) among all rules in
DR(a)
.
Because
r_{+}
is a total pre-order and because
a\preceq d
for all
d\in S_{0}\setminus\{a\}
, we can infer that for all
a_{l}\in\{a_{0},\dots,a_{m}\}
, for all
r_{a_{l}}\in DR(a_{l})
, we have
r_{a}\leq_{r}^{+}r_{a_{l}}
.
We now claim that for all
r_{c}\in DR(c)
,
r_{a}\leq r_{c}
must hold.
To see this, assume towards a contradiction that there is some
r_{c}\in DR(c)
s.t.
r_{c}<_{r}^{+}r_{a}
holds.
By transitivity of
\leq_{r}^{+}
, this implies
r_{c}<_{r}^{+}r
for all
r\in DR(b_{0})
.
This means
c\preceq b_{n}
.
Because
(c,b_{n})
is the result of a gen-rebut, we have
c\not\prec b_{n}
, i.e. either
c\not\preceq b_{n}
or
b_{n}\preceq c
.
Obviously
c\not\preceq b_{n}
contradicts
c\preceq b_{n}
, therefore we assume
b_{n}\preceq c
.
This means there exists some
r_{b}\in DR(b_{n})
s.t. for all
r_{c}^{\prime}\in DR(c)
,
r_{b}\leq_{r}^{+}r_{c}^{\prime}
holds.
Now for
r_{c}^{\prime}=r_{c}
in particular we can infer
r_{c}<_{r}^{+}r_{b}\leq_{r}^{+}r_{c}
, a contradiction.
With this, we have shown that for all
r_{c}\in DR(c)
,
r_{a}\leq_{r}^{+}r_{c}
holds.
Now we can infer that for all
r\in DR(c^{\prime\prime})
, we have
r_{a}\leq_{r}^{+}r
.
This, in turn, implies
a\preceq c^{\prime\prime}
, therefore
c^{\prime\prime}\not\prec a
and
(c^{\prime\prime},a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
as required.

Induction step:
n\rightarrow n+1
, i.e.
\mathcal{C}=\big\{(S_{0},b_{0}),\dots,(S_{n},b_{n}),(S_{n+1},b_{n+1})\big\}\subseteq{\Rightarrow_{+}}
.
Now
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\big\{(S_{1},b_{1}),\dots,(S_{n},b_{n}),(S_{n+1},b_{n+1})\big\}\subseteq{\Rightarrow_{+}}
is a support chain of length
n
.
By the induction hypothesis, there now exists an attack
(\widetilde{c},b_{0})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
with
L_{+}(\widetilde{c})=\textit{IN}
.
Since
\big\{(S_{0},b_{0})\big\}
is a support-chain of lengths
1\leq n
, we can now use the induction hypothesis again to infer that there is an attack
(\widetilde{c},a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
for which we have
L_{+}(\widetilde{c})=\textit{IN}
.

With this, we have shown that, if
a
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{+}
due to a support chain, then there also exists an attacker of
a
which is labeled
IN
in
L_{+}
.
As we have argued above, this implies that
a
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{i}
as required.
∎

Next, we will show that, when a labeling
L_{+}
of
\mathcal{J}_{+}
is reduced to a labeling
L_{i}
of
\mathcal{J}_{i}
, this reduced labeling is not only admissible, but even preferred.
For this, we first introduce a
combined labeling
similar to the combined minimal labeling of Definition
18
:

Definition 20
.

Let
AS_{1}=(R_{s_{1}},R_{d_{1}},n_{1},{\leq_{r_{1}}})
,
AS_{2}=(R_{s_{2}},R_{d_{2}},n_{2},{\leq_{r_{2}}})
be two
AS
s.t.
AS_{1}||AS_{2}
and let
AS_{+}
be their union.
Let
\mathcal{J}_{1}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{1}},{\preceq_{1}})
,
\mathcal{J}_{2}=(\mathcal{A}_{2},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{2}},{\preceq_{2}})
and
\mathcal{J}_{+}=(\mathcal{A}_{+},{\rightarrow_{+}},{\Rightarrow_{+}},{\preceq_{+}})
be the JSBAFs corresponding to
AS_{1}
,
AS_{2}
and
AS_{+}
respectively.
Furthermore, let
L_{+}\in pr(\mathcal{J}_{+})
be a preferred labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{+}
.
Lastly, for any
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
L_{i}=L_{+}\!\!\mid\!\!_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}
be the restriction of
L_{+}
to
\mathcal{A}_{i}
and let
L\in pr(\mathcal{J}_{i})
be a preferred labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{i}
s.t.
in(L_{i})\subseteq in(L)
.
We define the
combined labeling
of
L
and
L_{+}
, denoted as
L_{i+}
, as follows:

\displaystyle I_{i}

\displaystyle=in(L)

\displaystyle I_{old}

\displaystyle=in(L_{+})\backslash\mathcal{A}_{i}

\displaystyle I_{new_{0}}

\displaystyle=\big\{a\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})\mid CSub(a)\subseteq I_{i}\cup I_{old}\big\}

\displaystyle I_{new_{k+1}}

\displaystyle=\big\{a\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})\mid CSub(a)\subseteq I_{new_{k}}\big\}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\cup I_{new_{k}}

\displaystyle in(L_{i+})

\displaystyle=I_{i}\cup I_{old}\cup\bigcup\limits_{k\geq 0}I_{new_{k}}

\displaystyle O_{0}

\displaystyle=\{a\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\mid\exists(b,a)\in Att_{+}\textnormal{ with }b\in in(L_{i+})\}

\displaystyle O_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\big\{a\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\mid\exists(S,b)\in{\Rightarrow_{+}}\textnormal{ with }a\in S,

\displaystyle\hskip 17.07182ptS\setminus\{a\}\subseteq in(L_{i+})\textnormal{and }b\in O_{k}\big\}\cup O_{k}

\displaystyle out(L_{i+})

\displaystyle=\bigcup\limits_{k\geq 0}O_{k}

\displaystyle undec(L_{i+})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus\big(in(L_{i+})\cup out(L_{i+})\big)

Intuitively, the combined labeling of
L_{i}
and
L_{+}
can be obtained by labeling all arguments in either
L_{i}
or
L_{+}
as
IN
and then iteratively accepting all arguments in
\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
which need to be accepted to retain admissibility.
We will now show that this construction yields an admissible labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{+}
again.
As ususal, we have divided the proof into several parts to make it more accesible:

Proposition 30
.

Let
AS_{1}=(R_{s_{1}},R_{d_{1}},n_{1},{\leq_{r_{1}}})
,
AS_{2}=(R_{s_{2}},R_{d_{2}},n_{2},{\leq_{r_{2}}})
be two
AS
s.t.
AS_{1}||AS_{2}
and let
AS_{+}
be their union.
Let
\mathcal{J}_{1}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{1}},{\preceq_{1}})
,
\mathcal{J}_{2}=(\mathcal{A}_{2},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{2}},{\preceq_{2}})
and
\mathcal{J}_{+}=(\mathcal{A}_{+},{\rightarrow_{+}},{\Rightarrow_{+}},{\preceq_{+}})
be the JSBAFs corresponding to
AS_{1}
,
AS_{2}
and
AS_{+}
respectively.
Furthermore, let
L_{+}\in pr(\mathcal{J}_{+})
be a preferred labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{+}
.
Lastly, for any
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
L_{i}=L_{+}\!\!\mid\!\!_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}
be the restriction of
L_{+}
to
\mathcal{A}_{i}
and let
L\in pr(\mathcal{J}_{i})
be a preferred labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{i}
s.t.
in(L_{i})\subseteq in(L)
.
Then the combined labeling of
L
and
L_{+}
is a labeling.

Proof.

Let
L_{i+}
be the combined labeling of
L
and
L_{+}
.
Similar to the proofs of Proposition
13
and Proposition
25
, the actual proof proceeds via induction over the construction of
out(L_{i+})
.
However, it is clear that we only need to ensure
in(L_{i+})\cap out(L_{i+})=\emptyset
.
Furthermore, the induction step of this proof is again trivial.
We therefore only focus on the induction start, i.e. we show that there cannot be some
a\in in(L_{i+})\cap O_{0}
.
Towards a contradiction, suppose that this does not hold.
Then there are
a,b\in in(L_{i+})
s.t.
(b,a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
.

Note that both
a
and
b
are consistent arguments.
If
a,b\in in(L)
or
a,b\in I_{old}
, then this is easy to see by the admissibility of
L
and
L_{+}
.
In the case that we have
c\in\{a,b\}
with
c\in I_{new_{k}}\setminus(I_{i}\cup I_{old})
for some
k\geq 0
, then we can make a model theoretic argument to show the consistency of
c
:
Let
\{c_{i_{0}},\dots,c_{i_{k}}\}=CSub(c)\cap in(L)
and let
\{c_{old_{0}},\dots,c_{old_{l}}\}=CSub(c)\cap in(L_{+})\setminus\mathcal{A}_{i}
.
First, we construct the set
\mathcal{C}_{new}=\bigcup\limits_{c_{i}\in\{c_{i_{0}},\dots,c_{i_{k}}\}}ADSub(c_{i})
.
Since
L
is a preferred labeling, we can use Lemma
2
to infer that
\mathcal{C}_{new}\subseteq in(L)
holds.
Next, for each argument
c_{old}\in\{c_{old_{0}},\dots,c_{old_{l}}\}
, take
ADSub(c_{old})=\{\widehat{c}_{0},\dots,\widehat{c}_{p}\}
and let
AD_{c_{old}}=\{\widehat{c}_{0}^{\prime},\dots,\widehat{c}_{p}^{\prime}\}
be their reduced versions w.r.t.
AS_{i}
or
AS_{j}
(depending on the respecitve top-rules).
By
Lemma
2
we can infer that
ADSub(c_{old})\subseteq in(L_{+})
holds and by Lemma
3
this implies
AD_{c_{old}}\subseteq in(L_{+})
.

Now, take
\mathcal{C}_{old_{i}}=\big(\bigcup\limits_{c_{old}\in\{c_{old_{0}},\dots,c_{old_{l}}\}}AD_{c_{old}}\big)\cap\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
\mathcal{C}_{old_{j}}=\big(\bigcup\limits_{c_{old}\in\{c_{old_{0}},\dots,c_{old_{l}}\}}AD_{c_{old}}\}\big)\cap\mathcal{A}_{j}
(note that reduced versions of arguments are contained in either
\mathcal{A}_{i}
or
\mathcal{A}_{j}
by Corollary
3
).
By construction of
L
from
L_{+}
, we have
in(L_{+})\cap\mathcal{A}_{i}\subseteq in(L)
, therefore
\mathcal{C}_{old_{i}}\cup\mathcal{C}_{new}\subseteq in(L)
.
By admissibility of
L
, we can now infer that
(\mathcal{C}_{old_{i}}\cup\mathcal{C}_{new})^{C}
is satisfiable.
Similarly, we can use the admissibility of
L_{+}
to infer that
\mathcal{C}_{old_{j}}^{C}
is satisfiable.
By construction,
(\mathcal{C}_{old_{i}}\cup\mathcal{C}_{new})^{C}
and
\mathcal{C}_{old_{j}}^{C}
are syntactically disjoint sets of formulas, therefore there exists an interpretation
I
s.t.
I\vDash_{M}\bigwedge\big(\mathcal{C}_{new}\cup\mathcal{C}_{old_{i}}\cup C_{old_{j}}\big)^{C}
.
It is clear that we have
\big(\mathcal{C}_{new}\cup C_{old_{i}}\cup C_{old_{j}}\big)^{C}=ADSub(c)^{C}
.
Now let
\Gamma\subseteq Sub(c)^{C}
.
By Proposition
10
we can infer that
\big(\mathcal{C}_{new}\cup\mathcal{C}_{old_{i}}\cup\mathcal{C}_{old_{j}}\big)^{C}\vDash_{C}\Gamma
holds, therefore
I
is a model of
\Gamma
.
We conclude that each
\Gamma\subseteq Sub(c)^{C}
is satisfiable, therefore
c
is consistent.

Now for the actual proof:
Similar to the proof for Proposition
25
, we will show the claim by going through the different cases for the origins of
a
and
b
.
Note that for any
c\in\{a,b\}
, if we have
c\not\in(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
, we can infer from the construction of
in(L_{i+})
that
CSub(c)\subseteq in(L)\cup in(L_{+})
must hold.
Because both
L
and
L_{+}
are preferred labelings, this also implies
ADSub(c)\subseteq in(L)\cup in(L_{+})
by Lemma
2
.

Now for the different cases:
Because
L
and
L_{+}
are admissible labelings, it is clear that
a,b\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
, or
a,b\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
is a contradiction.
By Proposition
21
, we can also infer that
a\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
while
b\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
, and
a\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
while
b\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
both contradict the admissibility of
L
and
L_{+}
.
These two options correspond to case one of Illustration
1
.

Next, suppose that we have
a\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
b\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
, which corresponds to case two of Illustration
1
(the case
a\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
and
b\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
can be proven analogous).
Note that
b
is not necessarily a minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
-argument, as it might be the case that there is some
b^{\prime}\in CSub(b)\cap\big(\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})\big)
s.t.
b^{\prime}
is not a minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
-argument.
However, we can construct an
“alternative”
version of
b
which is a minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
-argument:
Let
\{b_{i_{0}},\dots,b_{i_{k}}\}=Csub(b)\cap\mathcal{A}_{i}
,
\{b_{j_{0}},\dots,b_{j_{l}}\}=CSub(b)\cap\mathcal{A}_{j}
and
\{b_{+_{0}},\dots,b_{+_{m}}\}=CSub(b)\cap\big(\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})\big)
.
For each
b_{+}\in\{b_{+_{0}},\dots,b_{+_{m}}\}
, let
b_{+}^{\prime}
be a reduced version of
b_{+}
w.r.t.
AS_{i}
or
AS_{j}
(depending on
TR(b_{+})
).
As we have mentioned above, we have
b_{+}\in in(L_{+})
, therefore we can use Lemma
3
to infer that
b_{+}^{\prime}\in in(L_{+})
also holds.
Note that each
b_{+}^{\prime}
is either in
\mathcal{A}_{i}
or in
\mathcal{A}_{j}
by Corollary
3
.
By construction of
in(L_{i+})
this also means that, if
b_{+}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
, then
L(b_{+}^{\prime})=\textit{IN}
.

Now we use
b_{+}^{C}=b_{+}^{\prime C}
, to construct a minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
argument
b^{\prime}
which is of the form
b^{\prime}:b_{i_{0}},\dots,b_{i_{k}},b_{j_{0}},\dots,b_{j_{l}},b_{+_{0}}^{\prime},\dots,b_{+_{m}}^{\prime}\rightarrow b^{C}
.
Obviously
b^{\prime}
undercuts or gen-rebuts
a
(depending on whether
(b,a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
was the result of an undercut or a gen-rebut).
In the first case, we can immediately infer
(b^{\prime},a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
, while in the second case we can use
DR(b^{\prime})\subseteq DR(b)
to infer that
b^{\prime}\not\prec a
and therefore again
(b^{\prime},a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
.
Either way, we now have that
b^{\prime}
is a minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
-argument which is attacking
a
.
By Proposition
22
, this implies that there is
b^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
s.t.
(b^{\prime\prime},a)\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
and
CSub(b^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}=CSub(b^{\prime\prime})
.
Because
b\in in(L_{i+})
by assumption and because
L
is closed under sub-arguments by Lemma
2
, we can infer
\{b_{i_{0}},\dots,b_{i_{k}}\}\subseteq in(L)
.
As we have argued above, we also have
\big(\{b_{+_{0}}^{\prime},\dots,b_{+_{m}}^{\prime}\}\cap\mathcal{A}_{i}\big)\subseteq in(L_{+})\cap\mathcal{A}_{i}\subseteq in(L)
.
Because
L
was a preferred labeling, we can therefore use Lemma
2
again to infer that
CSub(b^{\prime\prime})\subseteq in(L)
holds.
By admissibility of
L
we now have
b^{\prime\prime}\in in(L)
, contradicting
a\in in(L_{i+})\cap\mathcal{A}_{i}
.

Next, suppose that we have
a\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
while
b\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
(the case of
a\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
while
b\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
can be proven analogously).
Again, it is not necessarily the case that
a
is a minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
-argument, but we can construct an
“alternative”
version of
a
:
Let
\{a_{0},\dots,a_{m}\}=ADSub(a)
.
As we have argued above, we know that
\{a_{0},\dots,a_{m}\}\subseteq in(L)\cup in(L_{+})
holds.
Let
a_{0}^{\prime},\dots,a_{m}^{\prime}
be reduced versions of
a_{0},\dots,a_{m}
(w.r.t.
AS_{i}
or
AS_{j}
, depending on the respective top-rules).
By Lemma
3
we can infer that
a_{0}^{\prime},\dots a_{m}^{\prime}\subseteq in(L)\cup in(L_{+})
also holds.
By Corollary
3
we know that these arguments
a_{0}^{\prime},\dots a_{m}^{\prime}
are contained in
\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j}
.
Using Proposition
10
, we can now construct the minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
-argument
a^{\prime}
as follows:
a^{\prime}:a_{0}^{\prime},\dots a_{m}^{\prime}\rightarrow a^{C}
.
Note that, since we used
ADSub(a)
for the construction of
a^{\prime}
, we have
DR(a)=DR(a^{\prime})
.

Now, if
(b,a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
is the result of an undercut, then we can immediately infer that
(b,a^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
also holds.
On the other hand, if this attack results from a gen-rebut, then we can use Proposition
11
to construct the argument
b^{\prime}
of the form
b^{\prime}:b\rightarrow\lnot\bigwedge ADSub(a)^{C}
.
Since
ADSub(a)^{C}=ADSub(a^{\prime})^{C}
, we can infer
(b^{\prime},a^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
.
Either way, there exists an argument
\widetilde{b}\in in(L)
s.t.
(\widetilde{b},a^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
.
By Proposition
23
, we can now infer that one of the following two cases must hold:

1.

There are
a^{\prime\prime},\widetilde{b}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
s.t.
CSub(\widetilde{b}^{\prime})=CSub(\widetilde{b})
,
CSub(a^{\prime\prime})=CSub(a^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
and
(\widetilde{b}^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
.

2.

There are
a^{\prime\prime},\widetilde{b}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
s.t.
CSub(a^{\prime\prime})=ADSub(a^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
,
CSub(\widetilde{b}^{\prime})=CSub(\widetilde{b})
and either
(a^{\prime\prime},\widetilde{b}^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
or
(\widetilde{b}^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
.

By construction of
a^{\prime\prime}
, we have
CSub(a^{\prime\prime})=\big(\{a_{0}^{\prime},\dots,a_{m}^{\prime}\}\cap\mathcal{A}_{i}\big)\subseteq in(L)
and by admissibility of
L
this means
a^{\prime\prime}\in in(L)
must also hold.
Furthermore, we can use Lemma
2
and
b\in in(L)
to infer that
CSub(\widetilde{b}^{\prime})=CSub(b)\subseteq in(L)
must also hold.
By admissibility of
L
, this also implies
\widetilde{b}^{\prime}\in in(L)
.
Now both
(a^{\prime\prime},\widetilde{b}^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
and
(\widetilde{b}^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
contradict the admissibility of
L
.

Lastly, suppose that we have
a,b\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
, which corresponds to case four of Illustration
1
.
Analogous to the case above, we first construct minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
-arguments
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}
from
a
and
b
which are of the form
a^{\prime}:a_{0}^{\prime},\dots,a_{k}^{\prime}\rightarrow a^{C}
and
b^{\prime}:b_{0}^{\prime},\dots,b_{l}^{\prime}\rightarrow b^{C}
, where
a_{0}^{\prime},\dots,a_{k}^{\prime}
are reduced versions of the arguments in
\{a_{0},\dots,a_{k}\}=ADSub(a)
and
b_{0}^{\prime},\dots,b_{l}^{\prime}
are reduced versions of the arguments in
\{b_{0},\dots,b_{l}\}=ADSub(b)
.
Again, we can infer that
\{a_{0}^{\prime},\dots,a_{k}^{\prime}\}\subseteq in(L)\cup in(L_{+})
as well as
\{b_{0}^{\prime},\dots,b_{l}^{\prime}\}\subseteq in(L)\cup in(L_{+})
holds.
Furthermore, we can again infer that
(b^{\prime},a^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
must hold (possibly utilizing Proposition
11
as well as
DR(b)=DR(b^{\prime})
and
DR(a)=DR(a^{\prime})
, depending on whether or not
(b,a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
is the result of an undercut or a gen-rebut).
Then we can apply Proposition
24
to infer for the arguments
a^{\prime},b^{\prime}
that one of the following four cases must hold:

1.

There are
a^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
s.t.
CSub(a^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}=CSub(a^{\prime\prime})
,
CSub(b^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}=CSub(b^{\prime})
and
(b^{\prime\prime},a^{\prime\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
.

2.

There are
a^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
s.t.
CSub(a^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}=CSub(a^{\prime\prime})
,
CSub(b^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}=CSub(b^{\prime})
and
(b^{\prime\prime},a^{\prime\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{j}}

3.

There are
a^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
s.t.
ADSub(a^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}=CSub(a^{\prime\prime})
,
CSub(b^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}=CSub(b^{\prime\prime})
and either
(a^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
, or
(b^{\prime\prime},a^{\prime\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
.

4.

There are
a^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
s.t.
ADSub(a^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}=CSub(a^{\prime\prime})
,
CSub(b^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}=CSub(b^{\prime\prime})
and either
(a^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{j}}
, or
(b^{\prime\prime},a^{\prime\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{j}}
.

In each of these four cases we can infer a contradiction, since we either have
a^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime}\in in(L)
(cases one and three) or
a^{\prime\prime},b^{\prime\prime}\in in(L_{+})
(cases two and four).

With this, we have shown that each possible case for
a,b\in in(L_{+})\cap O_{0}
leads to a contradiction, as required.
∎

Proposition 31
.

Let
AS_{1}=(R_{s_{1}},R_{d_{1}},n_{1},{\leq_{r_{1}}})
,
AS_{2}=(R_{s_{2}},R_{d_{2}},n_{2},{\leq_{r_{2}}})
be two
AS
s.t.
AS_{1}||AS_{2}
and let
AS_{+}
be their union.
Let
\mathcal{J}_{1}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{1}},{\preceq_{1}})
,
\mathcal{J}_{2}=(\mathcal{A}_{2},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{2}},{\preceq_{2}})
and
\mathcal{J}_{+}=(\mathcal{A}_{+},{\rightarrow_{+}},{\Rightarrow_{+}},{\preceq_{+}})
be the JSBAFs corresponding to
AS_{1}
,
AS_{2}
and
AS_{+}
respectively.
Furthermore, let
L_{+}\in pr(\mathcal{J}_{+})
be a preferred labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{+}
.
Lastly, for any
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
L_{i}=L_{+}\!\!\mid\!\!_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}
be the restriction of
L_{+}
to
\mathcal{A}_{i}
, let
L\in pr(\mathcal{J}_{i})
be a preferred labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{i}
s.t.
in(L_{i})\subseteq in(L)
and let
L_{i+}
be the combined labeling of
L
and
L_{+}
.
We have
a\in out(L_{i+})
iff
a
is legally OUT w.r.t.
L_{i+}
.

Proof.

This proof is very similar to that of Proposition
26
.
We will therefore focus on the differences here.
As in the proof for Proposition
26
, the main task is to find an
“alternative reason”
for an argument
a
to be legally
OUT
in the case that
a
is labeled
OUT
in the construction of
L_{i+}
because of a support chain
\mathcal{C}=\big\{(S_{0},b_{0}),\dots,(S_{n},b_{n})\big\}\subseteq{\Rightarrow_{+}}
and an attack
(c,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
, where
a\in S_{0}
, for all
0<k\leq n
we have
S_{k}\setminus\{b_{k-1}\}\subseteq in(L_{i+})
, for
S_{0}
we have
S_{0}\setminus\{a\}\subseteq in(L_{i+})
and there is
d\in S_{0}\setminus\{a\}
s.t.
d\prec a
.
The proof idea is also the same as for Proposition
26
:
Let
S=S_{0}\setminus\{a\}\cup\bigcup\limits_{0<k\leq n}\big(S_{k}\setminus\{b_{k-1}\}\big)=\{d_{0},\dots,d_{m}\}
, let
c^{\prime}
be an argument of the form
c^{\prime}:c,d_{0},\dots,d_{m}\rightarrow\bigwedge\{c,d_{0},\dots,d_{m}\}^{C}
and for the set
ADSub(a)=\{a_{0},\dots,a_{l}\}
, let
a^{\prime}
be of the form
a^{\prime}:a_{0},\dots,a_{l}\rightarrow\lnot\bigwedge\big(ADSub(c)\cup ADSub(d_{0})\cup\dots\cup ADSub(d_{m})\big)^{C}
.
Note that by construction of
L_{i+}
, this implies
c^{\prime}\in in(L_{i+})
, from which we can also infer
CSub(c^{\prime})\subseteq in(L)\cup in(L_{+})
and – by Lemma
2
–
ADSub(c^{\prime})\subseteq in(L)\cup in(L_{+})
.
We have
(a^{\prime},c^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
and we use this as a starting point to infer that there either exists an attack or a support chain which satisfies the conditions for
a
to be legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{i+}
.
To do this, we go through the possible cases for the origins of
a^{\prime}
and
c^{\prime}
.

The cases for
a^{\prime},c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
(or
a^{\prime},c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
) and
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
while
c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
(or
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
while
c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
) are identical to those of Proposition
26
.
We therefore skip these cases and assume that we have
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
, which corresponds to case three of Illustration
1
(the case
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
and
c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
can be proven analogous).
Note that
c^{\prime}
is not necessarily a minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
-argument.
We therefore utilize a similar technique as in the Proof of Propsotion
30
and construct an
“equivalent”
argument to
c^{\prime}
as follows:
Let
ADSub(c^{\prime})=\{c_{0},\dots,c_{p}\}
and let

c_{0}^{\prime},\dots,c_{p}^{\prime}
be the reduced versions of these arguments (w.r.t.
AS_{i}
or
AS_{j}
, depending on the respective top-rules).
Then we construct the minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
-argument
c^{\prime\prime}
as follows:
c^{\prime\prime}:c_{0}^{\prime},\dots,c_{p}^{\prime}\rightarrow c^{\prime C}
.
Note that by using
ADSub(c^{\prime})
as a starting point, we have
DR(c^{\prime})=DR(c^{\prime\prime})
.
Furthermore, since
\{c_{0},\dots,c_{p}\}\subseteq in(L)\cup in(L_{+})
, we can use Lemma
3
to infer that
\{c_{0}^{\prime},\dots,c_{p}^{\prime}\}\subseteq in(L)\cup in(L_{+})
also holds.
By construction of
in(L_{i+})
, this also implies
c^{\prime\prime}\in in(L_{i+})
.
Lastly, we want to point out that both
a^{\prime}
and
c^{\prime\prime}
are consistent arguments.
For
a^{\prime}
, we can assume consistency since we could otherwise directly construct an attack towards the original argument
a
, while for
c^{\prime\prime}
we can assume consistency since otherwise we would be able to construct an attack from a strict argument towards
c^{\prime}
, which would contradict
L_{i+}(c^{\prime})=\textit{IN}
by construction of
L_{i+}
and Proposition
30
.

Now we can use Proposition
23
to infer that one of the following two cases must hold:

1.

There are
\widetilde{a},\widetilde{c}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
s.t.
CSub(\widetilde{a})=CSub(a^{\prime})
,
CSub(\widetilde{c})=CSub(c^{\prime\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
and
(\widetilde{a},\widetilde{c})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
.

2.

There are
\widetilde{a},\widetilde{c}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
s.t.
CSub(\widetilde{a})=CSub(a^{\prime})
,
CSub(\widetilde{c})=ADSub(c^{\prime\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
and either
(\widetilde{a},\widetilde{c})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
or
(\widetilde{c},\widetilde{a})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
.

Since
\{c_{0}^{\prime},\dots,c_{p}^{\prime}\}\subseteq in(L)\cup in(L_{+})
and since
c_{0}^{\prime},\dots,c_{p}^{\prime}
are the direct sub-arguments of
c^{\prime\prime}
, we can use Lemma
2
and the admissibility of
L
to infer that in either of the two cases,
\widetilde{c}\in in(L)
must hold.
This implies
\widetilde{a}\in out(L)
by admissibility of
L
.
By construction of
a^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{a}
we have
DR(\widetilde{a})\subseteq DR(a^{\prime})=DR(a)
as well as
ADSub(\widetilde{a})^{C}\subseteq ADSub(a^{\prime})^{C}=ADSub(a)^{C}
.
Since
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
by assumption and since
L
is admissible, we can now use Proposition
15
to infer
a^{\prime}\in out(L)
.
This means
a^{\prime}
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L
due to an attack or a support chain.
Because
DR(a^{\prime})=DR(a)
and
ADSub(a^{\prime})^{C}=ADSub(a)^{C}
, we can use the same attacker or support chain to infer that
a
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{i+}
(possibly with the help of Proposition
11
).

The case that
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j}
while
c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
(or
c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
) is identical to that of Proposition
26
and we skip it here.
This takes care of case two of Illustration
1
.
For the fourth and last case of Illustration
1
, i.e. both
a^{\prime}
and
c^{\prime}
are arguments in
\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
, we first need to construct minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
-arguments
a^{\prime\prime}
and
c^{\prime\prime}
as follows:
The argument
c^{\prime\prime}
is constructed as we did above, i.e. for
ADSub(c^{\prime})=\{c_{0},\dots,c_{p}\}
we use reduced versions
c_{0}^{\prime},\dots,c_{p}^{\prime}
to construct
c^{\prime\prime}
as
c^{\prime\prime}:c_{0}^{\prime},\dots,c_{p}^{\prime}\rightarrow c^{\prime C}
.
Analogously, for
ADSub(a^{\prime})=\{a_{0},\dots,a_{l}\}
, we use their reduced versions
a_{0}^{\prime},\dots,a_{l}^{\prime}
an construct
a^{\prime\prime}
as
a^{\prime\prime}:a_{0}^{\prime},\dots,a_{l}^{\prime}\rightarrow a^{\prime C}
.
Note that we have
\{c_{0},\dots,c_{p}\}\subseteq in(L)\cup in(L_{+})
.
By Lemma
3
this implies
\{c_{0}^{\prime},\dots,c_{p}^{\prime}\}\subseteq in(L)\cup in(L_{+})
, which in turn implies
c^{\prime\prime}\in in(L_{i+})
by construction of
L_{i+}
.
Since both
a^{\prime\prime}
and
c^{\prime\prime}
are minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
-arguments, we can now employ Proposition
24
to infer that one of the following four cases holds:

1.

There are
\widetilde{a},\widetilde{c}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
s.t.
CSub(\widetilde{a})=CSub(a^{\prime\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
,
CSub(\widetilde{c})=CSub(c^{\prime\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
and
(\widetilde{a},\widetilde{c})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
.

2.

There are
\widetilde{a},\widetilde{c}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
s.t.
CSub(\widetilde{a})=CSub(a^{\prime\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}
,
CSub(\widetilde{c})=CSub(c^{\prime\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}
and
(\widetilde{a},\widetilde{c})\in{\rightarrow_{j}}
.

3.

There are
\widetilde{a},\widetilde{c}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
s.t.
CSub(\widetilde{a})=CSub(a^{\prime\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
,
CSub(\widetilde{c})=ADSub(c^{\prime\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
and either
(\widetilde{a},\widetilde{c})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
, or
(\widetilde{c},\widetilde{a})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
.

4.

There are
\widetilde{a},\widetilde{c}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
s.t.
CSub(\widetilde{a})=CSub(a^{\prime\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}
,
CSub(\widetilde{c})=ADSub(c^{\prime\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}
and either
(\widetilde{a},\widetilde{c})\in{\rightarrow_{j}}
, or
(\widetilde{c},\widetilde{a})\in{\rightarrow_{j}}
.

Since
\{c_{0}^{\prime},\dots,c_{p}^{\prime}\}\subseteq in(L)\cup in(L_{+})
, we can use Lemma
2
to infer that
CSub(c^{\prime\prime})\subseteq in(L)\cup in(L_{+})
and
ADSub(c^{\prime\prime})\subseteq in(L)\cup in(L_{+})
holds, which in turn implies
\widetilde{c}\in in(L)
(if
\widetilde{c}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
) or
\widetilde{c}\in in(L_{+})
(if
\widetilde{c}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
) by admissibility of
L
and
L_{+}
.
In the first case, we can infer
\widetilde{a}\in out(L)
by the admissibility of
L
, while in the second case we can infer
\widetilde{a}\in out(L_{+})
by the admissibility of
L_{+}
.
Either way, we can use the fact that
\widetilde{a}
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L
or
L_{+}
and apply the same reasoning as before to infer that
a
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{i+}
.
Similar to our previous argumentations, this is possible because we have
DR(\widetilde{a})\subseteq DR(a^{\prime\prime})\subseteq DR(a^{\prime})=DR(a)
as well as
ADSub(\widetilde{a})^{C}\subseteq ADSub(a^{\prime\prime})^{C}\subseteq ADSub(a^{\prime})^{C}=ADSub(a)^{C}
.
This finishes the proof.
∎

Proposition 32
.

Let
AS_{1}=(R_{s_{1}},R_{d_{1}},n_{1},{\leq_{r_{1}}})
,
AS_{2}=(R_{s_{2}},R_{d_{2}},n_{2},{\leq_{r_{2}}})
be two
AS
s.t.
AS_{1}||AS_{2}
and let
AS_{+}
be their union.
Let
\mathcal{J}_{1}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{1}},{\preceq_{1}})
,
\mathcal{J}_{2}=(\mathcal{A}_{2},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{2}},{\preceq_{2}})
and
\mathcal{J}_{+}=(\mathcal{A}_{+},{\rightarrow_{+}},{\Rightarrow_{+}},{\preceq_{+}})
be the JSBAFs corresponding to
AS_{1}
,
AS_{2}
and
AS_{+}
respectively.
Furthermore, let
L_{+}\in pr(\mathcal{J}_{+})
be a preferred labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{+}
.
Lastly, for any
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
L_{i}=L_{+}\!\!\mid\!\!_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}
be the restriction of
L_{+}
to
\mathcal{A}_{i}
, let
L\in pr(\mathcal{J}_{i})
be a preferred labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{i}
s.t.
in(L_{i})\subseteq in(L)
and let
L_{i+}
be the combined labeling of
L
and
L_{+}
.
If
a\in in(L_{i+})
, then
a
is legally IN w.r.t.
L_{i+}
.

Proof.

Let
a\in in(L_{i+})
.
Similar to the proof of Proposition
27
, it is clear from the construction of
in(L_{i+})
that, for any support
(S,c)\in{\Rightarrow_{+}}
with
a\in S
and
a\preceq b
for all
b\in S\setminus\{a\}
, one of the items
1.a-1.c
of Definition
2
holds.
Thus, we only need to ensure that all attackers of
a
are labeled
OUT
in
L_{i+}
.
Let
(b,a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
.
Towards a contradiction, assume that
L_{i+}(b)\neq\textit{OUT}
.
Note that, if
L_{i+}(b)=\textit{IN}
, then
L_{i+}(a)=\textit{OUT}
by construction of
L_{i+}
, which contradicts
L_{i+}(a)=\textit{IN}
by Proposition
30
.
Therefore, we assume that
L_{i+}(b)=\textit{UNDEC}
holds.
As usual, we go through the possible cases for the origins of
a
and
b
to derive a contradiction in each of them.
Note that we can assume that both
a
and
b
are consistent, since they would otherwise be attacked by a strict argument, which again contradicts our assumptions on their labels (any strict argument
x
is contained in
\mathcal{A}_{i}
and accepted by the admissible labeling
L
which implies
x\in in(L_{i+})
and by construction of
out(L_{i+})
any argument
y
attacked by
x
is labeled
OUT
in
L_{i+}
).

It is obvious that
a,b\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
or
a,b\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
contradicts the admissibility of
L
or
L_{+}
.
Furthermore, if
a\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
b\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
, then this corresponds to case one of Illustration
1
and we can utilize Proposition
21
to infer that either
a
or
b
are inconsistent.
Again, this inconsistency would contradict our assumptions on the labels of
a
and
b
.
The case
a\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
and
b\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
can be proven analogous.

Next, suppose that we have
a\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
b\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
, which corresponds to case two of Illustration
1
.
If
(b,a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
is the result of an undercut, then this implies
Atoms(b^{C})\subseteq Atoms(AS_{i})
.
By Proposition
19
there now exists an argument
b^{\prime}
which is a reduced version of
b
w.r.t.
AS_{i}
.
By Corollary
3
we have
b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
, therefore admissibility of
L
yields
b^{\prime}\in out(L)
, which also implies
b^{\prime}\in out(L_{i+})
by
in(L)\subseteq in(L_{i+})
.
By Proposition
31
we know that
x\in out(L_{i+})
iff
x
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{i+}
, which means we can apply Lemma
3
to infer
b\in out(L_{i+})
, contradicting our assumption
b\in undec(L_{i+})
.
Now suppose that
(b,a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
is the result of a gen-rebut.
Then we can use Proposition
11
to construct
b^{\prime}
which is of the form
b^{\prime}:b\rightarrow\lnot\bigwedge ADSub(a)^{C}
.
By Proposition
19
there exists an argument
b^{\prime\prime}
whcih is a reduced version of
b^{\prime}
w.r.t.
AS_{i}
.
We have
DR(b^{\prime\prime})\subseteq DR(b^{\prime})
, thus we can infer
(b^{\prime\prime},a)\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
.
Now admissibility of
L
implies
b^{\prime\prime}\in out(L)
.
As in the undercut-case we can now use Proposition
31
in order to apply Lemma
3
and infer that
b^{\prime}\in out(L_{i+})
must hold.
This implies
b\in out(L_{i+})
by construction of
L_{i+}
, which contradicts our assumption on
L_{i+}(b)
.
The case
a\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
and
b\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
can be proven analogous.

Next, suppose that we have
a\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
while
b\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
.
This corresponds to case three of Illustration
1
.
If
(b,a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
is the result of an undercut, then this attack must also be directed towards some
a^{\prime}\in CSub(a)\subseteq in(L)\cup in(L_{+})
.
If
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
, then we can use the admissibility of
L
to infer that
b\in out(L)
holds and by construction of
L_{i+}
, this implies
b\in out(L_{i+})
, contradicting our assumption
b\in undec(L_{i+})
.
On the other hand, if
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus\mathcal{A}_{i}
, then we must have
b\in out(L_{+})
by admissibility of
L_{+}
, which also means
b\in out(L_{+}\!\!\mid\!\!_{\mathcal{A}_{i}})
.
By Proposition
29
,
L_{+}\!\!\mid\!\!_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}
is an admissible labeling, therefore there must exist an attack or support chain in
\mathcal{J}_{i}
, s.t.
b
is labeled
OUT
because of this attack or support chain..
By assumption, we have
in(L_{+}\!\!\mid\!\!_{\mathcal{A}_{i}})\subseteq in(L)\subseteq in(L_{i+})
, therefore
b\in out(L_{i+})
, contradicting our assumption
b\in undec(L_{i+})
.

Now suppose that
(b,a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
is the result of a gen-rebut.
Note that
a
is not necessarily a minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
-argument.
Similar to the proof of Proposition
31
, we therefore again use
ADSub(a)=\{a_{0},\dots,a_{m}\}
and their reduced versions
a_{0}^{\prime},\dots,a_{m}^{\prime}
to construct
a^{\prime}:a_{0}^{\prime},\dots,a_{m}^{\prime}\rightarrow a^{C}
, which is a minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
-argument.
By Lemma
2
we have
ADsub(a)\subseteq in(L)\cup in(L_{+})
and by Lemma
3
this implies
a_{0}^{\prime},\dots,a_{m}^{\prime}\subseteq in(L)\cup in(L_{+})
.
By construciton of
L_{i+}
we now have
a^{\prime}\in in(L_{i+})
.
Note that
DR(a)=DR(a^{\prime})
, therefore
(b,a^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
.
Now we can apply Proposition
23
to infer that one of the following cases must hold:

1.

There are
\widetilde{a},\widetilde{b}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
s.t.
CSub(\widetilde{a})=CSub(a^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
,
CSub(\widetilde{b})=CSub(b)
and
(\widetilde{b},\widetilde{a})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
.

2.

There are
\widetilde{a},\widetilde{b}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
s.t.
CSub(\widetilde{a})=ADSub(a^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
,
CSub(\widetilde{b})=CSub(b)
and either
(\widetilde{b},\widetilde{a})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
, or
(\widetilde{a},\widetilde{b})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
.

Since
a^{\prime}\in in(L_{i+})
, we have
CSub(a^{\prime})\subseteq in(L)\cup in(L_{+})
by construction of
L_{i+}
and by Lemma
2
we have
ADSub(a^{\prime})\subseteq in(L)\cup in(L_{+})
, which implies
\widetilde{a}\in in(L)
by admissibility of
L
.
Now, again by admissibility of
L
, we have
\widetilde{b}\in out(L)
.
Note that
CSub(\widetilde{b})=CSub(b)
implies
ADSub(\widetilde{b})=ADSub(b)
.
Since
\widetilde{b},b\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
and since
L
is admissible, we can now utilize Proposition
15
to infer
L(b)=\textit{OUT}
.
By admissibility of
L
this means
b
is labeled
OUT
in
L
either due to an attack or due to a support chain.
By
in(L)\subseteq in(L_{i+})
, we can infer that
b\in out(L_{i+})
due to the same attacker or support chain.
This contradicts our assumption
L\in undec(L_{i+})
.
The case
a\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
while
b\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
can be proven similarly.

Finally, suppose that
a,b\in\mathcal{A}_{+}\setminus(\mathcal{A}_{i}\cup\mathcal{A}_{j})
holds.
Again, we start by assuming
(b,a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
is the result of an undercut.
Then this attack is directed towards some
x\in CSub(a)\subseteq in(L)\cup in(L_{+})
.
If
x\in in(L_{+})
, then we can infer
b\in out(L_{+})
by admissibility of
L_{+}
, which implies
b\in out(L_{i+})
by construction of
in(L_{i+})
from
in(L_{+})
.
On the other hand, if
x\in in(L)
, then by Proposition
19
there exists
b^{\prime}
, which is a reduced version of
b
w.r.t.
AS_{i}
.
We have
b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
by Corollary
3
, therefore
b^{\prime}\in out(L)
by admissibility of
L
.
By construction of
in(L_{i+})
from
in(L)
, this implies
b^{\prime}\in out(L_{i+})
.
As before, we can now utilize Proposition
26
to apply Lemma
3
, and infer that
b\in out(L_{i+})
also holds.
This contradicts our assumption
b\in undec(L_{i+})
.

Lastly, assume that
(b,a)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
is the result of a gen-rebut.
As before, we use
ADSub(a)=\{a_{0},\dots,a_{k}\}
and the reduced versions
a_{0}^{\prime},\dots,a_{k}^{\prime}
of these arguments to construct the minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
-argument
a^{\prime}
which is of the form
a^{\prime}:a_{0}^{\prime},\dots,a_{m}^{\prime}\rightarrow a^{C}
.
Similarly, we use
ADSub(b)=\{b_{0},\dots,b_{l}\}
and the reduced versions
b_{0}^{\prime},\dots,b_{l}^{\prime}
of these arguments to construct the minimal
\mathcal{A}_{+}
-argument
b^{\prime}
which is of the form
b^{\prime}:b_{0}^{\prime},\dots,b_{m}^{\prime}\rightarrow b^{C}
.
Because
DR(a)=DR(a^{\prime})
and
DR(b)=DR(b^{\prime})
, we can infer
(b^{\prime},a^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
.
Similar to before, we can also infer
a^{\prime}\in in(L_{i+})
by Lemma
2
, Lemma
3
and by construction of
in(L_{i+})
.
Now we can apply Proposition
24
to infer that one of the following four cases holds:

1.

There are
\widetilde{a},\widetilde{b}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
s.t.
CSub(\widetilde{a})=CSub(a^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
,
CSub(\widetilde{b})=CSub(b^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
and
(\widetilde{b},\widetilde{a})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
.

2.

There are
\widetilde{a},\widetilde{b}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
s.t.
CSub(\widetilde{a})=CSub(a^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}
,
CSub(\widetilde{b})=CSub(b^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}
and
(\widetilde{b},\widetilde{a})\in{\rightarrow_{j}}
.

3.

There are
\widetilde{a},\widetilde{b}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
s.t.
CSub(\widetilde{a})=ADSub(a^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
,
CSub(\widetilde{b})=CSub(b^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{i}}
and either
(\widetilde{b},\widetilde{a})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
, or
(\widetilde{a},\widetilde{b})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
.

4.

There are
\widetilde{a},\widetilde{b}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
s.t.
CSub(\widetilde{a})=ADSub(a^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}
,
CSub(\widetilde{b})=CSub(b^{\prime})\!\!\mid\!\!_{AS_{j}}
and either
(\widetilde{b},\widetilde{a})\in{\rightarrow_{j}}
, or
(\widetilde{a},\widetilde{b})\in{\rightarrow_{j}}
.

Similar to before, we can infer by the construction of
L_{i+}
and by Lemma
2
that
a^{\prime}\in in(L_{i+})
implies
\widetilde{a}\in in(L)
(in cases 1 and 3) or
\widetilde{a}\in in(L_{+})
(in cases 2 and 4).
Either way, this again implies
\widetilde{b}\in out(L)
(in cases 1 and 3) or
\widetilde{b}\in out(L_{+})
(in cases 2 and 4) by admissibility of
L
and
L_{+}
.
Note that, by construction of
\widetilde{b}
and
b^{\prime}
, we have
DR(\widetilde{b})\subseteq DR(b^{\prime})=DR(b)
as well as
ADSub(\widetilde{b})^{C}\subseteq ADSub(b^{\prime})^{C}=ADSub(b)^{C}
.

We only show the case of
\widetilde{b}\in out(L)
here, as the case for
\widetilde{b}\in out(L_{+})
can be proven analogously:
Assume that we have
\widetilde{b}\in out(L)
because of an attack
(c,\widetilde{b})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
with
c\in in(L)
.
Regardless of whether this attack is the result of an undercut or a gen-rebut, we can use
DR(\widetilde{b})\subseteq DR(b)
,
ADSub(\widetilde{b})^{C}\subseteq ADSub(b)^{C}
, Proposition
11
and the admissibility of
L
to infer that there is an attack
(c^{\prime},b)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
from an attacker
c^{\prime}\in in(L)\subseteq in(L_{i+})
.
By construction of
L_{i+}
this implies
b\in out(L_{i+})
, contradicting our assumption
b\in undec(L_{i+})
.

Lastly, suppose that
\widetilde{b}\in out(L)
because of a support chain
\mathcal{C}=\big\{(S_{0},b_{0}),\dots,(S_{n},b_{n})\big\}\subseteq{\Rightarrow_{i}}
and an attack
(c,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
with
\widetilde{b}\in S_{0}
and
c\in in(L)
.
Similar to our proof for Proposition
26
, we can now use the set
S=\bigcup\limits_{0\leq k\leq n}S_{k}\cap in(L)
to create a new support chain
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\big\{(S\cup\{b\},b^{\prime})\big\}\subseteq{\Rightarrow_{+}}
.
If
(c,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
is the result of an undercut we can infer from
S\subseteq in(L)
that
(c,\widetilde{b})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
holds.
By
DR(\widetilde{b})\subseteq DR(b)
we then have
(c,b)\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
and by
c\in in(L)\subseteq in(L_{i+})
we can infer that
L_{i+}(b)=\textit{OUT}
, contradicting our assumption
L_{i+}(b)=\textit{UNDEC}
.
On the other hand, if
(c,b_{n})\in{\rightarrow_{i}}
is the result of a gen-rebut, then we can use Proposition
11
to construct an argument
c^{\prime}
which is of the form
c^{\prime}:c\rightarrow\lnot\bigwedge ADSub(b_{n})^{C}
.
Because
DR(c^{\prime})=DR(c)
,
DR(\widetilde{b})\subseteq DR(b)
and
ADSub(\widetilde{b})^{C}\subseteq ADSub(b)^{C}
, we can infer that we also have
(c^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in{\rightarrow_{+}}
.
By construction of
L_{i+}
, we have
c^{\prime}\in in(L_{i+})
.
By construciton of
out(L_{i+})
, we now have
b^{\prime}\in O_{0}\subseteq out(L_{i+})
and – because
S\subseteq in(L)\subseteq in(L_{i+})
–
b\in O_{1}\subseteq out(L_{i+})
.
This contradicts our assumption
b\in undec(L_{i+})
and finishes the proof.
∎

Proposition 33
.

Let
AS_{1}=(R_{s_{1}},R_{d_{1}},n_{1},{\leq_{r_{1}}})
,
AS_{2}=(R_{s_{2}},R_{d_{2}},n_{2},{\leq_{r_{2}}})
be two
AS
s.t.
AS_{1}||AS_{2}
and let
AS_{+}
be their union.
Let
\mathcal{J}_{1}=(\mathcal{A}_{1},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{1}},{\preceq_{1}})
,
\mathcal{J}_{2}=(\mathcal{A}_{2},{\rightarrow_{1}},{\Rightarrow_{2}},{\preceq_{2}})
and
\mathcal{J}_{+}=(\mathcal{A}_{+},{\rightarrow_{+}},{\Rightarrow_{+}},{\preceq_{+}})
be the JSBAFs corresponding to
AS_{1}
,
AS_{2}
and
AS_{+}
respectively.
Furthermore, let
L_{+}\in pr(\mathcal{J}_{+})
be a preferred labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{+}
.
Lastly, for any
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
L_{i}=L_{+}\!\!\mid\!\!_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}
be the restriction of
L_{+}
to
\mathcal{A}_{i}
, let
L\in pr(\mathcal{J}_{i})
be a preferred labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{i}
s.t.
in(L_{i})\subseteq in(L)
and let
L_{i+}
be the combined labeling of
L
and
L_{+}
.
Then
L_{i+}\in adm(\mathcal{J}_{+})
.

Proof.

By Proposition
30
we know that
L_{i+}
is a labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{+}
.
By Proposition
31
we know that
a\in out(L_{i+})
iff
a
is legally
OUT
w.r.t.
L_{i+}
.
By Proposition
32
we know that any
a\in in(L_{i+})
is legally
IN
w.r.t.
L_{i+}
.
By construction of
in(L_{i+})
, we have
in(L)\subseteq in(L_{i+})
, as well as
in(L_{+})\subseteq in(L_{i+})
.
By admissibility of
L
and
L_{+}
we can infer that
STR_{\mathcal{J}_{+}}\subseteq in(L_{i+})
holds.
We conclude that
L_{i+}
is an admissible labeling of
\mathcal{J}_{+}
.
∎

With this, we are finally ready to prove Theorem
3
.
We begin by proving that preferred semantics of Deductive ASPIC
⊖
satisfies non-interference:

Lemma 4
.

Preferred semantics of
Deductive ASPIC
⊖
